president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). kyoto runs until 2012, which is close, and there's nothing planned for after that. the follow-up seminar in bonn is unfortunately little more than a cover for the bleeding. the european union cannot wait any longer for states as reluctant as the united states, commissioner. we must take the lead ourselves, without cutting into our own flesh. we must, more than ever, have a policy of rewarding those industrial enterprises which invest heavily in environmentally friendly technologies. we must also make much greater efforts, particularly in the transport sector. mr florenz said it here at the beginning of the debate. the seventh framework programme for research and development offers excellent opportunities in this respect for setting up ambitious programmes. why not set the bar high and push forward concrete deadlines? why not, for example, have the ambition to have - i'll make it very concrete - 100% zero-emission road transport in europe by 2020? i have one last question, commissioner. do you agree that we should allocate additional resources, for example a zero tax rate - to put it that way - to encourage and promote the use of biofuels? commissioner, you can go down in history as the greatest friend of mother earth. i wouldn't miss that opportunity if i were you.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, a few weeks ago the former president of the european parliament, mr pat cox, gave the golden advice to the new and old members of this parliament that when a new matter comes up in parliament, they should apply the telephone test to it. this was the case with the public telephone boxes in ireland, which were allowed to continue to operate thanks to a european directive, because they were recognised as a service of general interest. it was immediately clear to the irish that we in this european parliament are also dealing with very concrete matters. i have also applied this test to the motor vehicle directive. i think it will pass this test with flying colors, because it is a directive which benefits the citizens. everyone involved in transportation, in traffic, is getting better. the directive has practical and positive effects which are important for citizens. i would like to draw your attention to a specific point which has already been raised in this debate. it is true that there is a deadline for the lodging of claims in the various countries, which, moreover, differs from one country to another. before a victim of a road traffic accident can bring a claim for damages, he must have the minutes of the proceedings. however, it sometimes takes months for this report to reach the victim. that is why i think what we have included in article 14 is so important. i would like to thank malcolm harbour, mrs wallis and medina ortega for the openness shown in reaching a compromise text on this. i think it is very important, however, that the member states do their utmost to provide the victim or his representative with the necessary information within the shortest possible time for the timely submission of the claim for damages. i think that the text that has been drawn up is a good compromise and i hope, as philippe whitehead has already said, that we can find the openness of the commission and the council to reach a solution that is satisfactory to all. i think this is a very serious problem and we must urgently address it with this directive.
president. - as vice-president of the convention, i can only welcome the fact that the european parliament will probably vote tomorrow in favour of this constitution by a very large majority. although the european parliament has not yet formally expressed its opinion on this, i believe that this statement, based on the excellent report by mr corbett and mr méndez de vigo, is an important signal to europe. we have indeed tried to provide an answer in the convention to the challenges facing europe today, namely enlargement and globalisation, and this constitution would not have taken on its present dimension without the important contribution of the european parliament. i think that this constitution, although a compromise, is qualitatively an important step forward. the constitution provides the institutional framework needed for an enlarged europe. europe cannot continue to operate with the same institutions and the same rules as when there were 15 member states. at the same time, i think that the real dimension of this constitution is that, although a first step has already been taken in maastricht, we are only now really moving from an economic community to a political union: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i hope that this constitution will be supported by a large majority tomorrow. but let us not forget that we as a parliament also have a great responsibility to convince the member states that europe needs this constitution. president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, together with the flemish greens i will be campaigning for the ratification of the belgian constitution in the federal, flemish and brussels parliaments over the coming months. tomorrow, however, we shall not be voting on the constitution, but on the report by mr corbett and mr méndez de vigo, and as far as i am concerned, it is a disappointing report. it's an apology, an overly one-sided enumeration of the many advantages of the constitution. what a missed opportunity, mr corbett! should we not have taken better account of the criticisms now being voiced on the left? why, for example, can it not be said that we are still on the way to a full-fledged european democracy, that the role of the constitutional regions has been underestimated, that too many policy areas are still left to intergovernmental cooperation, that in too many areas the veto still applies? why can it not be said that the european union is still too lacking in social order and too neoliberal in its orientation? i therefore ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to look again at the amendments tabled by the green group, in particular amendments nos 4, 9, 15 and 16, and perhaps to support them in the vote tomorrow.
mr president, like our group, the belgian socialist delegation will vote in favour of this report, which supports a text which is an important step on the road to european integration. the decision taken by parliament will be crucial, both democratically and politically, for the debates that will be held in the ratification process. for us it will be a 'yes', but not a definitive 'yes', but a genesis-like 'yes', in other words a 'yes' of struggle which must be a beginning and not an end. it will be a 'yes' because, as many of us have said, there are significant advances in this treaty. how can we not be glad that the constitutional treaty gives our parliament new and increased powers, thus imposing a democratic debate within the only european institution with the legitimacy of universal suffrage? how can anyone not agree with the incorporation of the charter of fundamental rights into the body of the constitutional treaty? it will be a "yes", but a "yes" that is both proactive and demanding. as regards the decision-making of the council of ministers, it will not have escaped the attentive reader that although the rule of double majority has been extended, that of unanimity is maintained for certain crucial decisions, among which are decisions in the social and fiscal fields. in the same way, some of us misunderstand the fact that a constitution contains and fixes in its text a programmatic part with which we cannot all agree. this text is not perfect. progress in certain social or fiscal matters will be difficult, but no more difficult than it is today. the important thing is to realize that this constitution is only a stage in the construction of europe. it only makes sense if it heralds an ambitious future project in which all european citizens, starting with the least well-off, can see and hope for an improvement in their living conditions. more than the constitution, the union will be judged on the subsequent acts it will or will not take, on its strength and political will to meet or not meet the hope of europeans to achieve or not to achieve the social europe, the europe of the people. that is what we must continue to give priority to.
commission. as the commission pointed out when it launched the poster campaign, the priorities of european commission development cooperation are: to reduce and eventually eradicate poverty; promote sustainable development; build democracies; contribute to the integration of developing countries into the world economy; support good governance and promote respect for human rights. the commission is committed to helping developing countries achieve the development goals of the millennium. however, there are important objectives and activities that go far beyond the millennium development goals. for example, promoting peace and security and also supporting institution building. a comprehensive, integrated approach is therefore essential in achieving sustainable development, as made clear also by the treaty on european union in article 3, which emphasises the importance of assuring consistency of external policies and instruments in the context of external relations, security, development, economic and trade policy. this is also clearly stressed in the 2004 annual report on the community's development policy. the community's external actions, including assistance, are further adapted to the diversity of our partner regions and countries. the european neighbourhood policy is an expression of such a comprehensive and integrated partner strategy. similarly, our relations with the larger developing countries in asia and latin america in particular cover a broad range of objectives. in this context, it is clear that bringing concrete benefits to our partners will make the world a better place, not only for the people directly benefiting from ec assistance but also for european citizens. however, this does not change the overriding objective of the development policy of the european commission, that is to eradicate poverty.

as you know, mr president, the european institutions have recently, in what i consider to be a fatal way, put a stop to the possible accession of a non-european country, turkey, to the european union. such a dossier threatens to change the whole nature of the union as we know it and even to put european cooperation as we know it on the fundamental precipice. it is a matter on which the vast majority of our electors, our citizens, are particularly alienated from the position taken by the official european institutions. i therefore regret that the luxembourg presidency does not take the opportunity to demand, firstly, respect for the democratic will of the majority of our citizens and, secondly, respect for the other small member state, cyprus, which is ignored and disregarded by the turkish leaders in the most arrogant and unacceptable way. i call this a missed opportunity, which can only widen the enormous gap between our citizens on the one hand and european politics on the other.
the fact that we reject this european constitution in the strongest terms does not mean that we are against far-reaching european cooperation. we are opposed to the european big brother state which is the exact opposite of what is in this constitution, namely respect for the principle of subsidiarity. we flemings live in the belgian federal state and we know firsthand how difficult it is, how impossible it is, to achieve a proper administration within a federal state. so if we now see that this constitution is making the european union increasingly resemble a kind of belgium in general, with far-reaching interventions in what we consider to be strictly national competences such as culture, language legislation, social security and so on, then that is more than one step too far for us. europe yes, european unity worst no. for these reasons and many others, we naturally voted against this european constitution.
mr president, it is of course not possible to list in the space of a minute all the reasons why i voted against the report on the european constitution. i shall therefore confine myself to the essential point, namely that a constitution is a matter for nation states and not for an institution like the european union. unless, of course, we intend to make the european union a federalist superstate, but then we do not have to count on the support of the flemish interest. not everything in the constitutional treaty is negative, on the contrary, but some simplifications and clarifications could have been made by means of a new classic european treaty. this constitution leads above all to new transfers of powers to the eu, to greater european involvement. moreover, the text is already obsolete before it has been ratified in the member states, in view of the likely accession of a country like turkey, which will fundamentally alter the functioning of all the institutions.
in writing. with the european constitution, europe wants to take steps towards greater european democracy and greater transparency. we are thinking here of the reinforced role of the european parliament in the decisionmaking process, the confirmation and consolidation of the principle of subsidiarity, the creation of a single legal framework and the simplification of legislative instruments. the extension of the european sphere of action in the fields of defence and asylum policy and the definition of european values are important new features in the constitution. the constitution is a step forward. a shy one, yes, but it's a forward one. the n-va therefore supports this step because it stands for a more democratic and transparent europe. a europe with a strong public culture, built from the ground up and supported by its citizens. but at the same time, the n-va also sees the constitution as a missed opportunity. three quarters of european legislation is implemented at a decentralised level, and yet the involvement of these implementing regional authorities is minimal. the european constitutional regions are not given any (formal) role in the european decision-making process. apart from a non-binding passage on the recognition of the national identity of the member states, the union also completely ignores the institutional reality in the various member states in this constitution. the continuing denial of this regional dimension is contrary to the idea of subsidiarity.
in writing. yes, i wholeheartedly support the draft european constitution and i voted enthusiastically for the excellent report by my colleagues. a draft treaty which, while it is a compromise, contains considerable progress. the union of 25 becomes stable with a president appointed for two and a half years instead of the rotating presidency every six months. a foreign minister who will be the voice of europe in the world. the charter of fundamental rights is finally incorporated into the institutional corpus. citizens who have a right to petition (translatable into european law from one million signatures). and in the social field, for the first time, the union has set itself the objectives of common progress in a social market economy. in short, a more democratic, more transparent, clearer and more efficient european union. but perhaps the hardest part is still to be done: to bring ratification to fruition in all our member states and thus to explain and convince the citizens of europe. and i am glad that in these times of euro-frivolity, on the constitutional treaty, belgian citizens, according to a recent poll, are in the forefront of the fight for yes: 80% are in favour!
in writing. the corbett/méndez de vigo report can be classified as 'pure propaganda' and is not a fair assessment of the pros and cons of the present convention. it does not mention that too many policy areas are still left to intergovernmental cooperation and that in important areas the veto still applies. the role of the constitutional regions in the european decisionmaking process remains precarious. part iii of the treaty makes it clear that the union lacks a social order and confirms the choice of the neoliberal model. the european union is not yet a security union and is in danger of putting more emphasis on the use of military means than on the use of conflict prevention. these remarks are not in the report, which is why i am voting against. this does not prevent me from campaigning with the greens! in the coming months for the ratification of the treaty in the federal, flemish and brussels parliaments. the constitution is a huge step forward and if we do not ratify it we will fall back on the provisions of the treaty of nice, which are less efficient, transparent and democratic than the present treaty establishing a european constitution.
in writing. i am very convinced, mr president, that i voted in favour of the resolution and therefore in favour of the constitutional treaty. the arguments which a large majority of the european parliament endorses, namely to encourage a positive attitude on the part of the citizens of europe and the national and regional parliaments, are also my own. i also share the criticism of the fact that untruths are being spread about this constitutional convention. the text which now needs to be democratically ratified only contains steps in the right direction: it will provide more opportunities to work in an open, transparent, democratic and effective way in those policy areas where the union can and must provide added value for citizens. he deserves all our support.
it is of the utmost importance, mr president, that we should consult from time to time on transatlantic relations. our relations with the united states have not improved in recent years. there is no doubt that this has a lot to do with the american intervention in iraq, but there are other problems as well. we have to remember the unacceptable pressure exerted by the us governments, by the us president himself, to have turkey join the european union. i do not think that it is in the interests of the transatlantic dialogue for one side to interfere in the internal political affairs of the other. that said, we must also dare to say that there is a certain kind of primary anti-americanism in this parliament too, which does not make things any easier. europe and the united states share the same values, such as democracy and human rights, the free market economy, and so on. they are often called universal values, but they are in fact western, let us say european, values. we also share a number of common enemies. firstly, there is the threat of islamic terrorism, even if we differ on how to deal with this problem. in this respect, we must not dwell too much on multilateralism versus unilateralism. in practice, multilateralism often means that we hide behind institutions like the united nations, so that in practice little or nothing happens. our weakness in military matters and in foreign policy is not the fault of the americans, but is the fault of ourselves alone. we can still learn from america, just think of the tsunami in southeast asia. american military personnel were very quick to arrive on the scene to provide actual assistance. the european union was present in the person of commissioner louis michel, who came to check on the damage and handed out a few bags of rice to the television cameras. the contrast was stark and telling.

we are discussing a resolution on the commemoration of the holocaust, anti-semitism and racism. it would have been expected that a text would pay tribute to the victims of the holocaust, with a serene appeal to never forget this horrible episode in european history, so that such a thing could never happen again. my party, the flemish interest, could of course have agreed to such a resolution, along with 99% of the members of the european parliament, but the resolution under discussion here is not in that spirit. in a shameful way, the horrors of the second world war are being juxtaposed with, and i quote, the rise of extremist and xenophobic parties and the growing acceptance of their views by public opinion. the resolution, in paragraph 5, calls for the current fight against racism, xenophobia and anti-semitism to be placed in education against the background of the shoah. my party, the flemish interest, is neither extremist nor xenophobic, but the politically correct terminology is indeed directed against the contemporary democratic political parties which are fighting for the preservation of national identity. the traditional political groups simply want to demonize a number of successful and growing competitors at the expense of the victims of the holocaust. in this way, not only are millions of voters in europe offended, but, what is worse, the horrors of totalitarian mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur, mr costa, on his report and the excellent conclusions he has presented, which were almost unanimously approved by the committee on civil liberties. i think this report is a strong signal from parliament to the commission and the council. it is a sign of our determination to ensure a higher quality of justice for all european citizens, and i would even say for all those who are in the territory of the european union, thanks in particular to the charter on the quality of criminal justice and the proposed evaluation system. mutual recognition of decisions in criminal matters in one or other member state presupposes mutual trust by all states in their judicial systems. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial instruments are used in a proper manner. but it's not just the procedure. ensuring and verifying that judges are well trained, open, balanced, available, efficient, diligent and able to manage existing resources in an optimal way is of course not in contradiction with the indispensable independence they must enjoy. the transparency of justice must be improved and citizens must be better informed about the working methods of judges, who sometimes suffer a loss of credibility and trust. finally, i think it is essential that our recommendations be followed up. we need action, not just intentions, commissioner. it is therefore important to set up a monitoring committee composed of experts, judges, legal practitioners, users of justice and representatives of national parliaments whose task is to assess and evaluate the way in which our recommendations are implemented. if we succeed in putting these recommendations into practice, we will be able to make the european union a true state governed by the rule of law. these are fundamental questions for the functioning of our democracies, for the respect of the law and for the rights of citizens. as regards the excellent report by mr di pietro, i fully agree with the analysis and proposals contained therein, but i would like to stress that the proposal made is modest, confined to the current legal framework, which dates back to 1959, and therefore does not meet the information needs of criminal records. i think we were trying to respond to the emotion of the fourniret case. we will look forward, as commissioner frattini has announced, to more comprehensive proposals, which are essential to combat terrorism, serious crime and crime in general more effectively. the small step we have taken does not exempt us from taking a further step quickly.
in the explanatory memorandum to its proposal, the commission points out that recent tragic cases of paedophilia have shown that there are major problems in the exchange of conviction data between member states. commissioner frattini has just mentioned the name. the fournier case is in fact being referred to. the french authorities have not considered it necessary to provide the necessary information on the person concerned. the proposal now under discussion has taken far too long to be put to the vote. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the application of the principle of equal treatment for men and women. the proposal is also inadequate and fails to address a large number of problems. member states should complete and make available their criminal records more quickly, and provide data requested by other member states more quickly, using standard forms. these are some steps in the right direction, but of course it remains to be seen whether a fully automated system of data exchange will be introduced. the commission must now make a move as quickly as possible, especially since it is already stating that the system will not be able to function for a number of years, which in itself is a bad sign. there are, of course, a number of legal implications which we shall have to discuss in detail. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission itself provides a striking example of how this is clearly not the case. it does not provide for the obligation of a member state to inform the state of residence of a convicted person if that state is not the state of which he is a national. in other words, these measures will allow the aforementioned fourniret to slip through the net again.
i agree with those who say that the commission's proposals are a first step and that the report in the field is a first step and a good report. we have too much legal uncertainty at the moment for local actors. we leave it to the court of justice at this stage to decide what exactly needs to be done in the matter of state aid. that's a first step, but at the same time, it's not enough for us. the exemption of compensation paid to hospitals and social housing is a first step, but we think others should be added to the list. we are thinking, commissioner, of health care institutions, we are thinking of employment services which we think should also be excluded from the notification. then there is this fourth criterion in the altmark judgment. we are not going to accept this and it is not practical. how, after all, is the average well-managed private enterprise to be determined? what to do when there is no private equivalent? the reference to the average private undertaking is not too minimalist in its interpretation of the public service remit. the desire to present itself as a well-run private enterprise can also have repercussions in terms of redundancies and social drama. i therefore ask that this criterion be deleted.
on behalf of the group. mr president, commissioner, a well-known french economist has just published a book entitled 'the politics of impotence', and i think that is a sentiment that many of our fellow citizens can legitimately feel. i would add, however, that this policy of impotence is in fact a deliberate strategy chosen since maastricht, since 1992, which is broadly based on three lines: structural reform of the markets - as we call it in our jargon - reduction of public deficits and a policy aimed at conquering external markets. in addition, from the institutional and competence-sharing point of view, the european union has exclusive competence in competition law and monetary union, and for the rest the member states have to manage and compete with each other. i think that 15 years later - and i regret to say this - this institutional choice is reinforced by the constitutional treaty, which i approve, even though it contains many shortcomings. fifteen years later, the results are in line with expectations, or rather the lack of results in terms of growth, and above all the quality of growth, and in terms of job creation. so our message as an environmental group, commissioner, is that we really need to change our strategy. we have the impression that the discussions on the stability pact are marginal and that, in reality, if we really want to achieve better results, we need to put in place a more voluntary policy at the euro area level, in which ministers finally share a european support framework around more ambitious objectives and where efforts are made to achieve convergence between the different national economies. since i have little time, i will mention three of these goals. the first, i believe, is a massive taxation of labour and alternative ways of financing social security systems, taking into account the diversity of countries. but i think it is very important to be able to say to all citizens that those who work, those who have the chance to work, must be able to live decently from their work. and i think that achieving that goal requires a massive taxation of labor. second objective, and you will not be surprised, we need quality growth, which is more energy efficient and much less polluting. look at the commission's documents on the evaluation of the community strategy for sustainable development: the results are disastrous, hence the absolute need to change course from this point of view. thirdly, we must support internal demand within the union. the focus should not be on conquering external markets, as if the economy were made up of only large companies or innovative small and medium-sized enterprises targeting the chinese and indian markets. we must support internal demand, which means public and private investment and the dissemination of a clear message from public authorities, both at the level of the european union and at the level of the member states, that we are proud to be european, that we have built a monetary union, as well as an internal market. we need a real economic and social union now, one which gives everyone a future.

the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would first of all like to thank very warmly all my colleagues here who have shown great interest in this important subject. i would first of all like to thank the fictitious rapporteurs, antonios trakatellis, dorette corbey, bart staes, bairbre de brún and then, of course, all the colleagues whose very valuable cooperation and collaboration i have appreciated throughout the preparation of this report. the debate was intense in all the political groups and, i would say, lived up to the challenges and expectations. 89% of european citizens say they are particularly concerned about the impact of the environment on their health and with good reason! the figures are encouraging, and i will mention only a few: one sixth of child deaths and illnesses in europe can be attributed to environmental factors; the so-called allergic respiratory diseases have doubled in the last 20 years, affecting one in seven children today. almost 10% of workers are exposed to carcinogens; 14% of couples consult for difficulty conceiving. i could, as i said, multiply the figures and examples here and, by the way, my colleagues did not fail to do so during the debates. in response to these figures, the european commission launched the scale strategy for environment and health in june 2003, scale for science children's awareness legal instruments evaluation. this initiative has raised a lot of hopes, as was well summed up by margot wallström's statements in our plenary debate in march last year. i quote her: 'there are some areas where we cannot risk waiting for our knowledge to be complete but where we must act on the precautionary principle, and we will.' in short, this european strategy was to serve as a launching pad for the present action plan for which i am the rapporteur. but it is not. this action plan, i would say, is not what it is called, but rather a programme for the assessment of the overall environmental impact on health. of course, there are a number of interesting clues. they are worth mentioning and remembering. i would mention in particular the actions to raise awareness of the risks faced by citizens, the training of health and environmental specialists - there are far too few of them - the introduction of biomonitoring at european level, all of which are interesting initiatives, as i said, but which do not give off the sense of unfinished business that i felt when i read the commission's communication. indeed, an approach which makes absolute scientific proof the paradigm of the 21st century seems to me to be a departure from the application of the precautionary principle. the 52 european ministers for the environment and health who met in budapest last june for the conference on 'a future for our children' understood this by stressing the balance, the delicate but necessary balance, between more research and the urgency of preventive action to protect health. it is this balance that we must seek and find here. that is why we have made exposure to hazardous chemicals a priority in this report. in particular, in paragraph 6, we call for the precautionary principle to be applied to a whole series of substances harmful to health, in particular certain heavy metals such as mercury and cadmium, six products of the phthalate family used in pvc plastics and four insecticides suspected of being, among other things, endocrine disruptors and causing serious malformations. strengthening this european plan also means, i would say, drawing on the ambitious actions already being successfully implemented by the member states. i refer to paragraphs 23 and 28 of the resolution. i am glad that my colleagues support my approach to putting children at the heart of the system, in particular by calling on the commission to carry out an epidemiological study of children from birth to adulthood. another crucial point is the fight against passive smoking. the commission has taken account of this concern, and we welcome this. it wants to classify tobacco smoke in the environment as a class 1 carcinogen. let us be clear on this point: i cannot in any way compromise the message we are sending to the citizens, which is the subject of paragraph 20 of the resolution. tobacco smoke kills not only smokers but also non-smokers. in belgium alone, there are 2,000 victims of secondhand smoke a year, as many as in road accidents. in conclusion, i hope that the european commission will be ambitious in implementing this action plan for the next six years. i have no doubt, mr dimas, that you will work hand in hand with your colleague, mr kyprianou, to ensure the necessary complementarity between the action plan and the european public health programme. you can of course count on our support in parliament to secure funding to meet the challenges i have just mentioned, not only in the seventh research framework programme but also in chapter 4 of the financial perspective. i hope you will also support the introduction by your services of a green paper on indoor pollution, from the nursery to the home and the school, which is still the poor relation of environmental legislation. all this is to say that europe must learn from the tobacco and asbestos scandals, to name but a few. europe is in a race against time against all forms of pollution. commissioner, you and we have a duty to ensure that the health of the citizens, of all citizens, takes precedence over all other considerations.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, many people are concerned about the impact of the environment on their health, and these are very concrete matters. meanwhile, it has been sufficiently demonstrated that fertility declines and miscarriages increase due to environmental influences. it's becoming increasingly clear that people suffer from allergies. the number of children suffering from asthma and having to go through life with a puffer, a syringe, is growing. the impact of tobacco smoke on the health of non-smokers is very clear to all of us. the action plan therefore fits into the scale approach which focuses on five key concepts: the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the establishment of a european centre for the development of vocational training (ecvet). the presentation of the action programme, commissioner, was full of disappointment and skepticism. can i mention three things? member of the commission. madam president, i wish to begin by thanking you all for your constructive remarks in both the report and during the debate today. the contribution of the european parliament is very important. through the close cooperation between parliament and the commission we will be able to provide the european union with an effective and efficient way of addressing the possible consequences on human health of environmental concerns. i wish to comment briefly on your observations and try to answer your questions. the gradual setting-up of an integrated environment and health information system, in which human bio-monitoring will play an essential role, is the added value of the action plan. it is a long-term and ambitious plan, but this will not prevent us from taking action. indeed, many actions have already been taken on the major environment and health issues. just to give you some examples, i wish to draw your attention to clean water and sanitation, which are dealt with through the urban waste water treatment directive, the drinking water directive and the bathing water directive. respiratory diseases will be tackled even more through cafe - the clean air for europe programme. diseases related to chemical and physical stresses are already addressed through the legislation on pesticides and biocides. the new chemicals policy - reach - will be an essential cornerstone in this area. we need to bear in mind how far we have come and ensure that we build on existing efforts rather than duplicate them. there is already a great deal of legislative action forthcoming which will have an impact on the protection of human health. this relates in particular to the thematic strategies of reach and cafe. we must continue with our efforts before coming up with additional elements and it is for this reason that the environment and health strategy focuses on preparing the next generation of environmental legislation. the environment and health strategy contained a specific focus on children and during the stakeholder consultation process to prepare the action plan, experts highlighted that concern. firstly, that parental exposure should be included to cover the unborn child and, secondly, that we should look at adults in order to cover diseases caused by childhood exposure. for these reasons the action plan has widened the focus to include adults as well, taking into consideration the various categories of vulnerability. the precautionary principle is at the core of environment policy and it will continue to be the cornerstone of our action. it will be applied according to the conditions set out in the relevant legislation. the commission fully agrees with this line. i want to be extremely clear to avoid any possible misunderstanding. the commission will not use this lack of information in the field of environment and health as an excuse for inaction. regarding the financial plan, we very much welcome parliament's support in its role as budgetary authority for the funding to implement the action plan. we are constrained by the existing budgetary initiatives until 2006 and we will make the best use of all available funds. we are currently making the case within the commission for a very substantial increase in the amount of research funding devoted to environment and health. parliament's request for funding of eur 300 million for 2007-2010 provides us with extremely useful political support. however, the member states must also play a role. a european union version of the united states' national children's study, which the parliament has requested, would cost about eur 100 million per year. this amounts to a massive increase of our current environment and health funding. we hope to provide as much support as possible at european union level, but major commitment must also come from the member states. the commission greatly values parliament's cooperation and its constructive efforts in this area. in order to make the most of this the commission will regularly inform the relevant parliamentary committee on the progress made in implementing the action plan. finally, i would like to underline that by 2007 we will have made significant progress in establishing the information system and we hope to have the first results of the effectiveness of cost efficiency measures to address health problems. at this point we will report back to parliament in substantive terms on whether existing policies are working and what new policy initiatives will be required.
the commission's vice-president madam president, ladies and gentlemen, on 21 october 2003 the commission adopted a proposal for a directive on driving licences, which you will be discussing in a moment. today, more than 110 different types of driving licence, granting different rights for certain types, are valid and in circulation in the member states. in an open space where the free movement of citizens is the rule, the consequences of this situation are obvious. the controls of this variety of licences are very difficult, public authorities have difficulty finding their way there, citizens often have problems of non-recognition of their driving licences. this situation is no longer acceptable. the directive proposed by the commission has three main objectives: anti-fraud protection, free movement and road safety. as regards fraud protection, the commission proposes to eliminate the paper model. from the date of application of the directive, the only community model that can be used will be the plastic card. this will also help to reduce the number of models in circulation. the member states will have the possibility of inserting a microprocessor in the driving licence which will, of course, contain only the elements shown on the plastic card and cannot be used for any other purpose. this will help to strengthen the protection against fraud. the commission also proposes to introduce a time-limited period of validity of the licence. this administrative validity, which will be ten years for car and motorcycle licences, will make it possible to update the anti-fraud protection elements at each renewal and to have a recent photo on the document. this is the real way to avoid a situation comparable to the present situation. the introduction of the limited period of validity will put an end to the last obstacle to free movement in this field. citizens moving to another member state will no longer have to deal with different periods of validity in different member states. this is the element which, with the harmonization of the model permit, makes it possible to remove the obstacles to free movement. the commission proposal also aims to improve road safety. to this end, the commission proposes to introduce a new category of driving licence for mopeds. motorcycle riders are the youngest motorised road users. they are also particularly vulnerable, as all the statistics show, in that they are proportionately much more involved in accidents than other users. the commission also proposes to regulate the right to drive the most powerful motorcycles progressively, to do the same for heavy goods vehicles and the most powerful coaches, to harmonise the frequency of medical examinations for professional drivers and to introduce minimum requirements for the initial qualification and continuing training of driving licence examiners. there you have it, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, an ambitious proposal to improve road safety, to ensure free movement, to combat fraud in driving licences. it will certainly have an impact on a number of citizens for whom the driving licence is a guarantee of mobility and free movement and an identity document in everyday life.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i would like to begin by thanking all those involved, both the commission and the administration of parliament, but also my colleagues, because for some months now the directive on driving licences has been the subject of a search for coherence and also of a great deal of dialogue, because this issue has not so much given rise to partisan differences as to sensitivities which have varied greatly from one country to another. it is in this context, mr commissioner, that i would like to thank the commission for its enlightened advice, and also all the people who have invested themselves heavily in the subject. we have sought consensus and coherence, but we have also made sure that the whole thing adds value for the citizens, since it is, after all, about almost two thirds of the european citizens who are concerned. that is why we have taken a small step further: instead of 111 driving licences plus one european model, the proposal from the committee on transport and tourism is that we should have a single european driving licence from 111 within a reasonable period of about 10 or 20 years. this is of course symbolic, but it also has the advantage of simplifying controls. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right of initiative in the matter of the internal market. the internet is full of them, so the countries are also mentioned here. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the added value for citizens also means legal certainty. we do not want to undermine the rights we have acquired, on the contrary, we want to strengthen them. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rights of professional drivers are not lost, for example when they move to another place, as we have seen in the cases which have been brought before the commission. it is also about the citizen as a tourist who travels from one country to another and wants to see his rights recognized there too. we have also opted not to discriminate on the basis of age, and accordingly we leave it to the countries to introduce medical tests or other tests that are purely preventive. but i am convinced that what is already the norm in some countries will also expand. the added value is also greater road safety; the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of training. training is the basic element and does not prevent further training. in this respect we have sought consistency with the directive on training for professional drivers, whether in bus or truck transport. we have also solved the problem of motorhomes and caravans, for example, by not introducing a more complicated b+e driving licence, but by adapting training; the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster. the idea was also based on the progressive access for motorcyclists, and you mentioned this in the introduction: we are all aware that efforts can and must be made in this area. if we are to count today the total number of road deaths, which is over 40,000, then it is clear that the number of deaths in the car sector is decreasing, but unfortunately not in the motorcycle sector. here, the numbers are alarmingly consistent. that is why we are proposing a gradual approach, but we do not want to rely on theoretical examinations. we are counting on training and, accordingly, we would also like to give the countries that want to set a lower starting age the possibility that legal certainty is guaranteed for their citizens, but that the progression remains in the directive. we do not want to challenge this tradition in the various countries, which is also linked to mobility and the economic situation, but we want to have a general concept with a european average age, and we want to focus on continuing training. there are and will be problems of equality. we have started a first attempt to include three- and four-wheel vehicles, but we also know that this cannot be done overnight. i personally, however, remain of the opinion that the equalisation of car and motorcycle licences is not advisable because driving behaviour is fundamentally different, although perhaps the understanding of one road user for the other could be improved. so, in short: simplification, legal certainty, road safety and fraud prevention - these are the main aspects we wanted to present in this proposal. i would like to thank all the members for the very sincere cooperation i have enjoyed over the past few months.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party president, commissioner, fellow rapporteur, i must congratulate you. you have had a very difficult task, you have dealt with something which involves almost all citizens but which is also very technical, with many details and great differences between member states. your task was all the more difficult because everyone accepts the principles and says we must have a simpler system, better controls, etc., but nobody really wants to give up national differences. we are driving more and more on each other's roads, so we must also trust each other more and more in the papers we give to drivers, but we still regard the giving of that paper as a kind of strategic, almost military secret which each member state must keep for itself and where the stamps must therefore be kept in the strictest secrecy. i think that your assignment has made it very difficult and that it has not made our job any easier. my group is in favour of a european model, of course. we are in favour of this as soon as possible and therefore of regular administrative renewal. we are not in favour of any further tests being carried out. it's an administrative innovation, even for older people. the statistics don't say that. there is no indication that older people are particularly unsafe drivers. and besides, for a lot of older people, a car is just a social necessity, so let's not make it harder for them to drive. the same should apply to sick people. ideally, the decision to drive should be made between a patient and his doctor, with the member states and are not too restrictive. we support the general lines of your report, mr rapporteur. in any case, we consider this report to be a major step forward. that we take a step towards a car or of a truck. i think that people are also aware that the european union is also there for very concrete things. i think your report is a step forward in that direction and i thank you for it.

president. - ladies and gentlemen, from 14 march to 22 april the united nations commission on human rights, so praised by some here, will be holding its 61st annual session in geneva. we can only hope that the un commission will be guided only by strict ethical and objective criteria and will no longer be influenced by considerations which have more to do with cynical realpolitik than with the defence of human rights. unfortunately, this has happened all too often in the past and it is clear to everyone that the united nations in general and the un commission in particular are operating with double standards. the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter, and i am afraid that the commission will not be able to take a decision on it. last year, for example, the resolutions on zimbabwe, which the european union helped to table, and the resolution on china were rejected at the 60th session of the un commission on human rights. as far as these countries are concerned, the un went so far as to adopt what is known as a no-action motion. it is not the case that in some cases, i am thinking of china, the concern of un bureaucrats for human rights decreases proportionately with the economic interest of the countries concerned. we are not prepared to accept it, but it is a fact, particularly in the case of china, that some people have a double standard. china is the world champion of death sentences and executions, but that does not seem to deter several eu member states from setting aside the great ethical principles and remaining silent on human rights when chinese leaders are visiting europe. naturally, important contracts for airbus, for example, have to be put on hold. and then, of course, it's more advisable to keep quiet about the troublesome dalai lama or the troublesome taiwanese. and the same double standard is being applied to turkey, and this has of course been mentioned several times in recent months by speakers from various political groups, given the current situation. accession negotiations with turkey had to be and would be opened, and despite the continuing reports of numerous tortures, the suppression of freedom of expression and assembly, state negationism in connection with the armenian genocide, and the continued occupation of northern cyprus, it was not true that turkey met the copenhagen criteria. these are just two examples to show that in this world realpolitik still prefers the great democratic and ethical principles to which it gives lip service. neither the un nor our own eu are credible apostles and moral philosophers in this area.
mr president, someone once described the un commission on human rights in geneva as the annual human rights circus. in recent years it has been shown on several occasions that the sheets in geneva are handed out by countries which are themselves known human rights violators. by forming strategic alliances, they keep each other's hands above their heads and even manage to pat each other on the shoulder. a fundamental reform of the composition of the un commission and of the voting procedures is necessary. otherwise, she risks perishing from her own unworthiness. the european union also has a great responsibility in this respect. it must strive for greater coherence and consistency in its human rights policy. the union must finally take a real lead, so that the 61st session is not a superfluous show of strength but an efficient and credible debate leading to fair and, above all, concrete results.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, the fight against hunger and poverty is and will of course remain one of the greatest challenges of our time. and it is rightly said that there is a link between aid, debt relief and trade and that action in these three areas should be complementary. i should like to concentrate on the commercial aspect here today. being able to engage in the global economy offers benefits to all countries, whether rich or poor. as a concrete example, we could put comparable countries side by side, for example, south versus north korea. or the asian tigers against the south asian countries. a free, fair and development-oriented multilateral trading system is indeed an effective mechanism for reducing poverty and hunger. the european union in particular must ensure better access to the world market for the poorest countries, by giving priority to capacity building and technical assistance, so that trade-led development is made possible to the greatest extent possible. the next wto ministerial meeting in hong kong in december will be a crucial test of the willingness of the rich countries to give the poor countries a full opportunity to participate fully in world trade. doha has been proposed as the development round and after cancun we cannot afford a second failure. further liberalization of world trade is in any case a basic condition for more economic growth, both here and in the developing countries. alongside aid and alongside debt relief, properly liberalised trade is a very important, if not the most important, instrument for distributing the world's wealth more evenly and more equitably and for reducing the gap between rich and poor.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, it is well known that a particularly aggressive dumping policy, with unlikely price reductions of up to 70% below the price of raw materials, has already led to china taking over one third of europe's quota-free textile imports. if nothing is done, a european market which is now fully open will be flooded with cheap textiles from china, but also from india and pakistan. i have serious doubts, commissioner, whether the european commission's action plan of 13 october 2004 will be enough to help the european textile industry in its fight for survival. the plan provides for support measures but does not contain any trade-related measures. the plan would have monitored textile imports into europe from 1 january so that the commission could intervene quickly if necessary. but commissioner mandelson acknowledged last week in the committee on international trade that this monitoring system is still not operational and that criteria have not even been set. when will this finally be the case? what if it's too late? what if the calf drowned? i would urge the commission to use all possible means to combat the unfair trade practices of some countries. the commission has the means to do this, such as a horizontal safeguard clause, a textile-specific safeguard clause permitted under the multifibre arrangement in certain circumstances or, in the extreme case, complaints to the wto dispute settlement panel. in short, commissioner, what we expect from the commission is a more trade-oriented and a more aggressive approach. we must have the courage to take a stand against our major trading partners, especially when they are unfairly competing with us. the 177,000 companies and 2.7 million workers in the european textile industry are not being left in the cold.
president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, allow me to confront you with a statement by your dear colleague, mr mandelson. he recently said the following to the french newspaper "le figaro". i quote: "according to an oecd study, the abolition of textile quotas will save each family €270 a year". now, madam president, your colleague mr mandelson has unfortunately forgotten the thousands of families who will lose or have lost their jobs as a result of the abolition of quotas and the accompanying chinese 'flood wave'. mr mandelson is also very short-sighted because he is, whether consciously or not, overlooking the fact that the expected price reductions will only concern a very limited range of textile and clothing products, so will have a very limited effect in general. i am surprised and particularly disappointed by the ultra-liberal discourse of mr mandelson, but i will leave that aside. i have a few specific questions and suggestions. firstly, you can promise here that the commission will react and take action if it is proved that chinese products are dumped on the european market. this can be demonstrated by the prices of raw materials on the international markets. the commission must only have the will to do so. secondly, commissioner, what safeguard measures - and we have already spoken about them here - are you considering to prevent certain categories of products from being wiped out in europe as a result of unfair competition? finally, what concrete steps can you take, and will you take, to ensure that the basic, social and ecological thresholds are also respected in china? for example, you could make certain practices public in the context of a name and shame information campaign, to name a few. finally, commissioner, we are also informed that imports of textiles from china increased significantly, even spectacularly, last month. i hope that the commission will not now come up with the killer again, that our textile industry has had 10 years to adapt, because that would be the height of simplicity.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, ladies and gentlemen, on behalf of the epp group i would like to say that we are generally satisfied with the report adopted by the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. i can assure you, madam rapporteur, that we will unconditionally re-endorse the package of amendments, all nineteen of them. we have all worked together on the subject of unfair trading practices for a long time and as the work has progressed, from the green paper to now the second reading, we have become increasingly aware of the need to make the internal market not just an area where the free movement of goods and services simply takes place, but also an area where that free movement takes place with the assurance that all of that is in accordance with fair practices. it is not, of course, that until now, or until the directive comes into force, everything will be unfair. the member states have also taken their responsibility in the past when it was convenient for them, but we have found that as long as those member states arrange for such protection in a different way, we are faced with a problem in the internal market and in particular a problem of lack of confidence in that internal market. the confidence of the consumer who is not comfortable with it and therefore does not dare to look across the border, the confidence of the companies too, and i am thinking in particular of the smallest companies, which also have difficulty in crossing the border because they too do not know what the rules are on the other side of that border. i think, mr president, that the work we have done together deserves to be seen. it is a very balanced piece, it provides a high degree of consumer protection as the treaty expects us to do, but it also provides maximum legal certainty for the trader and the two together provide a perfect balance. we are also taking a cautious step towards full harmonization for the first time. i think we can do that in this one because we go very far in consumer protection. all this has been possible because we have worked together perfectly, perfectly with colleagues from other groups, perfectly with the people of the council, with the people of the commission. i would like to thank all those who were involved, because it is only because of that openness, transparency and constructive attitude that we have reached this point and that we can conclude the dossier at second reading. i would like to thank you, madam rapporteur, for your efforts in this area. president. - although this proposal concerns only b to c relations, we in the epp are satisfied that this legislative document explicitly states that if an undertaking finds that a competitor is infringing the directive, that fact can be used by that undertaking to make it a case of unfair commercial practices, a case of distortion of competition, in accordance with the national law of the country in question. we are also pleased that we have finally been able to identify the reference consumer amongst ourselves, that we are taking the average consumer as a reference point but that we are doing so without losing sight of the vulnerable consumer. i think we've found a very good balance there, too. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
president. - i have two minutes to make two specific points, both of which have to do with synergy. the commissioner, you know better than we do that the impact of the research programmes will be much greater if you succeed in bridging the gap between the research and development programmes under cohesion policy on the one hand and the seventh framework programme on the other. if these two instruments are coordinated, the result will be far more profound. let me give you an example from my own region, the border area between the netherlands, germany and belgium. the universities of eindhoven, leuven, aachen and also hasselt and maastricht are already working closely together on research, training and development, including in the context of cohesion projects. i believe that such crossborder cooperation projects deserve to be emulated and encouraged, because they strengthen the regional dimension of the lisbon process. the role of the cities, the universities and the regions in which they operate cannot be questioned, can it? we are therefore very pleased, commissioner, that an amendment to this effect by mr van nistelrooij has been adopted in the commission on the locatelli report which was adopted by parliament earlier this week. parliament expressly calls for cooperation with the regions and for a link to be established between the national programmes under the structural funds and the programmes under the seventh framework programme. the commissioner, to conclude, is aware that companies are ready to invest, to take advantage of the opportunities that arise. if the eu, if we are now financially underfunded, if we do not put our big words about innovation policy into action, then our credibility is, of course, in jeopardy, yours and ours, i think. we are also giving the national authorities an ideal excuse, the perfect excuse, to not use the national resources that are so necessary to get across the bridge. at the moment, there is what is called a window of opportunity, and many governments at all levels, regional, national and also european, of course, are convinced that we need to drastically increase our investment in innovation. if we let this momentum pass us by, i think we're just gonna have to look in our own boots.
the green group shares everyone's concern to ensure that the livelihoods of the fishing communities in the tsunami-affected areas are safeguarded and strengthened, but, ladies and gentlemen, the regulation which is being put forward is a bad idea, a bad proposal. we have heard the opinions of the wwf, we have heard the opinions of local ngos and i have also asked the fao for an opinion. turns out, the boats we're trying to get to are fit like a pig's tongue. there is a difference in structure, there is a different way of fishing and inappropriate aid can be counterproductive. there will also be problems in keeping the boats we are planning to send operational. in addition, the fao confirmed to me that most of the fisheries in that region are overfished and that we must take account of the limited biological capacity on the spot. adapted fishing techniques are an absolute necessity. on top of that, ladies and gentlemen, the fao also stresses that there is no demand from the affected countries to import old or new boats. the indonesian and sri lankan governments have even told the fao that this action is undesirable. the governments and the fishing communities would much rather have support to build their own boats, adapted to local traditions and fishing methods. the fao confirms that there is sufficient local technical capacity to build these boats locally. in short, this is unwanted and inappropriate support. the commission has even stated that it will accept only three of the 21 amendments which we have approved. that is why, ladies and gentlemen, that is why the president of my group voted against this proposal for a regulation by a large majority.
mr president, the opening of the so-called parliament of communist china was marked by the announcement of a vote on an anti-secession law. it is a threat to taiwan, which, as we all know, is a democratic country which has been de facto independent since 1941. at the same time, the communist regime announced an increase in military spending of 12.6%. it's hard to separate one fact from another. the european council is preparing to lift the arms embargo against china at this very moment. this is a decision which would be totally unacceptable if europe is to remain credible in relation to human rights in the world. it is therefore desirable that a strong signal should be sent from this parliament in favour of maintaining the arms embargo and against the threat of aggression by the so-called people's republic of china against democratic taiwan.
