the general system of preferences, which grants developing countries reduced import tariffs for the export of their products to europe, is the most concrete application of the principle of 'development by trade'. this system accounted for over 500 billion euros of exports from developing countries to europe last year. the report sets out the basic principles for the next ten years. i still believe, however, that this proposal for a regulation is too limited a translation of the objectives previously set by the commission. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. there would be more clarity, more flexibility and, above all, more transparency, including a rapid review procedure where necessary. i do not think that these objectives have been sufficiently achieved. the reform is a step in the right direction, but it is clearly not going far enough. the alde group therefore abstained from voting in the final vote in the committee on international trade and today tabled five amendments. these amendments have been deliberately kept to a minimum. they are reasonable and are aimed primarily at providing greater guarantees that the priority objectives are actually being achieved. our group wants the system to benefit those countries which really need it, and to protect our own industry against unfair or unfair competition. what we are seeking with our amendments is simply to prevent abuse or misuse of the system. i therefore sincerely hope that the rapporteur and our colleagues will be able to meet our concerns so that we too can vote in favour of this report when we vote later, or tomorrow.
president. - mr commissioner, minister, ladies and gentlemen, the review conference on the non-proliferation treaty is in a few weeks' time and it is not too soon, because in recent months the emphasis has been so much on real and sometimes alleged weapons of mass destruction that attention to nuclear proliferation would have been slackened if the developments in north korea and iran had not awakened us. there is a real danger that the non-proliferation regime will erode to the point of losing all meaning. with all the risks of a renewed, uncontrolled arms race. and that in a world that has become much more opaque and much more dangerous because of international terrorism. so this debate and the joint resolution before us are, as i said, not a moment too soon. my group calls on both the eu institutions and the member states to form a united front at the review conference and to make a strong plea for new initiatives on disarmament in general and nuclear disarmament in particular. since we count among our member states both nuclear and non-nuclear powers, this may not be so obvious. that is why i welcome the council's announcement that it is working hard on a common position for that conference. we also call on all third countries and all eu member states which have not yet done so to ratify the additional protocols to the international atomic energy agency as soon as possible. in the same vein, it is of the utmost importance that the comprehensive test ban treaty should come into force as soon as possible. this requires the ratification of a number of states, including, not least, the united states. as for the united states, we do not understand why it is constantly, and rightly so, warning of the dangers of weapons of mass destruction and at the same time working on a new generation of so-called 'light' nuclear weapons. there is no doubt that such a development will trigger another arms race, this time for a type of weapon that could fall into the wrong hands even more easily. we therefore call on the united states to abandon these plans and join us in devoting all its energies to the revitalisation of non-proliferation and disarmament efforts.
president. - the worrying developments in iran and north korea require us to speak with one voice as the european union. it also obliges us to work closely with the united states and all the other countries which are actually behind the non-proliferation treaty. it is of course important that there should be negotiations with north korea and iran and that the dialogue can still continue. but if it turns out that the dialogue does not produce concrete and satisfactory results, other means must be used if necessary, because states like north korea and iran, which possess nuclear weapons, are a threat to world peace. we must also ask ourselves, in the case of iran, why that country really needs nuclear energy, even for peaceful purposes. it is also politically disturbing that a violation of the non-proliferation treaty can be abused in practice to enforce all kinds of favourable terms. the treaty must be reinterpreted in such a way that high-risk states can no longer engage in nuclear enrichment and processing tout court.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, international terrorism, organized crime and the unpredictable behaviour of some states mean that the risk of nuclear conflict is still very high. the monitoring capacity of the international atomic energy agency should therefore be strengthened as a matter of urgency. the european union must have the courage to take political and economic action against countries that break the rules and try to circumvent controls. it must show the necessary ambition and commit itself to full implementation of the convention, especially at the next review conference. i also hope that the union can play an important role in persuading north korea to return to the negotiating table through dialogue and consultation. the united states, russia and france have begun the development of a new generation of nuclear weapons. however, a genuine commitment to non-proliferation and disarmament means that everyone, and not just the so-called rogue states, will put an end to the research and development of weapons of mass destruction. if this does not happen, all the statements on the subject will remain empty rhetoric.
mr president, will lebanon be one of those countries in the orange revolution whose people's awakening rings the bell of democracy? we wish. but lebanon is not like ukraine or georgia. it will not be easy, or even desirable, to sever the ties with syria sealed by the ta'if agreements. but these links must be clarified, cleaned up, and the popular push and the international pressure are working on it. what is clear today is that the killers of rafic hariri and the 14 lebanese citizens who died in the bombing are still on the run. the syrian army must be able to withdraw from lebanon without losing face, but the redeployment of its troops cannot be confined to the bekaa plain, while the timetable for the final withdrawal has not yet been set. the organisation of the next parliamentary elections is now a test of the will to change in this country. it is also because hezbollah remains a major player in syrian politics and is actually undermining the peace negotiations between israel and palestine, as mr morillon has said. but that will be the case as long as israel does not withdraw its troops from the golan, as the un has asked it to do through various resolutions. finally, the opposition, by uniting and wanting to proceed step by step, shows an astonishing maturity and shows that it is well in control of the pre-revolutionary situation that has been created. we can go along with this movement, but we cannot rush it. the european parliament must therefore be particularly vigilant in maintaining pressure on syria to fulfil its international commitments. everyone - lebanon, the european union, syria - knows how close the game is here. the shadow of war still hangs over beirut, but at the same time, the opposition in this country has never seemed so united. we know too little about this opposition, mr president. i would suggest that we use the coming weeks to deepen our ties with her. i would not like to end without expressing my condolences and my sympathy and friendship to mrs hariri, who will undoubtedly also be one of the actors in lebanon's future.
member of the commission. mr president, i am pleased to be here to set out the commission’s intentions on the services proposal. i have taken note of the reports referring to a possible withdrawal of the proposal by the commission. i can confirm to you that the commission has no intention of withdrawing the proposal currently on the table. based on my consultations and having listened carefully to all sides of the argument, i identified a number of areas where i believe changes would be required for the broad consensus i would like to see emerge. many of you have urged me to clarify the commission’s position on these points in the interest of facilitating parliament’s own deliberations. members may recall that during my hearing before the european parliament, i promised to consult and listen carefully before making any pronouncements on the draft directive, which the previous commission brought forward. since then i have been meeting extensively with meps, social partners and ministers. i was heartened by the degree of support there is across the political spectrum for opening the services market. if we want to achieve the levels of growth necessary for sustainable development then a vibrant services sector is a prerequisite. yet, the single market in services is often still a distant ambition, rather than a reality. if we are to let our economies develop and grow we must maintain a clear focus on the services market. the services directive is a key element in our efforts to relaunch the lisbon agenda. getting the full benefit that services can bring to the eu economy will boost growth and jobs. if we are serious about the lisbon agenda, we must be serious about the services market. after my initial round of contacts, i went to president barroso and said that i believed the current proposal would never be adopted unless we were prepared to accept modifications. that is why in presenting the commission’s views on the lisbon strategy to you some weeks ago, president barroso indicated that the commission believed some changes would be necessary to ensure success. opening up the services market is an ambitious project. the proposal to achieve this is innovative. to be successful we need a broad consensus. there are aspects of the current draft that are giving rise to serious concerns. the debate had become too polarised. many of you have raised such concerns and it is clear that shortcomings have to be addressed. i had the opportunity to outline my views briefly to the conference of presidents last week and i am happy to discuss this further with you today. let me reiterate the areas i have identified. the directive will have to be clear that conditions and standards for workers will not be affected in any way. the text will have to be watertight on this point. there will be no change to the existing situation in this regard. i do not want to hear any more talk about so-called 'social dumping'. this is not what this proposal is about and we should put an end to this confusion. another area i have identified is the exclusion from the scope of the directive of sectors such as health and publicly funded services of general interest: for obvious reasons, people regard these sectors as particularly sensitive. we should address concerns about the operation of the country of origin principle. we need to maintain this if we want to promote the cross-frontier provision of services. to do so we will need to address key issues such as giving greater confidence and certainty to businesses and consumers on what law will apply to cross-border transactions. we also need to build the trust and confidence between member states necessary for it to operate effectively. the above points reflect the principal areas emerging from my own consultations. it is of course up to you in parliament to make your own decisions. clearly, it is your responsibility to decide on the amendments you want to bring forward. on behalf of the commission, i am merely signalling our willingness to work openly and constructively with you. yesterday at the council of ministers for competitiveness there was an exchange of views on the services proposal. the presidency welcomed the clear statement that no new proposal would be presented by the commission. it endorsed the suggestion that health and social services of general interest be excluded from the proposal. it agreed that the current text needed to be modified if it was to be adopted. i hope we can now move the discussion in the european parliament and council onto the positive elements that this proposal can bring. many aspects of the proposal have met with broad support and we should build on these. removing the red tape currently strangling efforts to establish or provide services on a cross-border basis will make a real contribution to entrepreneurship, growth and job creation. ensuring that companies have easy access to information on requirements they have to comply with will contribute to this, as will the simplification of authorisation procedures. service providers and their customers and consumers should not see their desire to benefit from the single market frustrated by being subjected to discriminatory and disproportionate requirements. recipients of services should also have easy access to information on service providers and their services. it should be easier to choose a service provider from another member state. quality of services should be guaranteed and simple access to dispute settlement must be available. we need to develop the cooperation, confidence and assistance between member state administrations. this is what the single market in services is about. it will increase competition, stimulate entrepreneurship and provide new opportunities for service providers, which will give a much-needed boost to the eu economy. by ensuring that we deliver on this we will give the services economy the boost it requires. we will fulfil the commitment set out in the lisbon agenda. i am prepared to be constructive and positive. from the meetings i have had with many of you, i have learned a great deal and very useful and constructive suggestions have been made. i remain open and available to all of you to discuss particular ideas, concerns or proposals you may have. i believe that with this i have outlined the commission’s position and our desire to work loyally within the codecision process. (applause)
member of the commission. mr president, the commission is grateful for this opportunity to make a statement on the proposal on the patentability of computer-implemented inventions. i have already had the opportunity to discuss the proposal with the committee on legal affairs on 2 february and with the conference of presidents on 3 march. on both occasions i took very careful note of parliament’s views. i noted that parliament considers, in general, that its views were not given sufficient weight at first reading. from the debate in the legal affairs committee, i noted that there are differing views on the substance of the proposal, in particular its content and purpose. the commission gave careful consideration to parliament’s request of 24 february, submitted under article 55 of parliament’s rules of procedure. but the commission concluded that at that stage, regrettably, it could not submit a new proposal as parliament requested. that was not because the commission wished to persist stubbornly with the proposal, but because the council was on the point of adopting a common position. as i explained to the conference of presidents, the council reached a political agreement in may 2004 at first reading. the council has been on the verge of confirming the political agreement in the form of a common position since december 2004. the commission had supported the political agreement of may 2004. the commission could not, therefore, go back on its word when the council was in the process of confirming its common position. the council has now made up its mind and adopted its common position. it did so yesterday at the competitiveness council. jeannot krecké, chair of the competitiveness council, has already explained to the legal affairs committee the reasons behind the council’s stance. it confirmed its common position, primarily for institutional reasons. the council wanted to avoid the setting of a precedent, with member states being seen to back away from a deal they had signed up to in may 2004. the council confirmed its position to show that a deal is a deal and that it was not creating a logjam in a key area for innovation. yesterday, when the council took its decision, jeannot krecké noted that some member states had concerns about the substance of the text and that these would be addressed at second reading. the ball is now very clearly in the european parliament’s court. it is for you to decide how you want to play it. i do not have to remind you of parliament’s rights: we discussed this in the conference of presidents. you can, of course, reject or substantially amend the proposal. if parliament decides to reject it, then the commission will respect your wishes. i will not propose a new directive. should you decide to propose amendments, the commission will give them due consideration. no doubt improvements can be made. you will understand, of course, that i cannot speak on behalf of the council and i would urge parliament to engage constructively with the council on this subject in the future. i am ready to help in any way. before concluding, i would like to say a few words on the substance of the proposal, since parliament will now need to turn its attention to this. the commission proposed to clarify the legal rules on patentability for software-related inventions. this does not include computer programs or other software as such. it means inventions that make a technical contribution and that are truly novel. such inventions are present in a number of everyday consumer goods such as cars, mobile telephones and domestic appliances. the commission’s intention in making its proposal was to avoid the patenting of pure software and make a clear distinction between the european union and the united states. nothing that is not patentable now will be made patentable by the directive. the current rules in the european patent convention leave patent examiners very wide decision-making powers. there can be different interpretations as to whether an invention can be patented. this leads to uncertainty for businesses. small and medium-sized enterprises in particular are negatively affected by the lack of clarity in the existing rules. i would like to remind members that, in the absence of a directive, patents will continue to be granted. if patent offices decide to grant patents for pure software, then expensive procedures before the courts will be the only option for those who wish to object. those of you who have been directly involved in working on this proposal know as well as i do that this is a very complex area. any modifications will need to be carefully examined. the directive cannot be turned on its head. we need to maintain a proper balance between stimulating innovation and making sure competition is not stifled. i hope i have not spoken for too long. the situation is now clear. the ball is in your court. i am sure you will exercise your rights and your member of the commission. mr president, human tissues and cells have become an important part of healthcare. their therapeutic use continues to increase, not only in terms of the frequency of use of tissues and cells, but also in the range of ways in which they can be used. tissues and cells are often acquired through cross-border exchanges. over the past decade, the interchange of these human substances, both within europe and between europe and third countries, has increased. the fact that the exchange of tissues and cells can result in the transmission of disease has focused attention on the need for increased safety measures. the european institutions responded to this concern by adopting a directive in march 2004 establishing quality and safety requirements for human tissues and cells. the directive ensures that an increasing number of patients in europe treated with human tissues and cells can trust that these substances are safe and of good quality. that constitutes yet another step forward in fulfilling the mandate in article 152 of the treaty, which is to set high standards of quality and safety for substances of human origin. the directive has to be transposed by 7 april 2006 in all member states. on that date, it must, therefore, be applied in all member states, including the united kingdom, and will also be part of the community acquis to be implemented by romania under the enlargement process. i would like to underline a key point. article 152, upon which the directive is based, aims to regulate public health issues. as was made clear during the debate in parliament leading up to the adoption of the directive, although fundamental ethical principles can be raised and endorsed, it is not possible to have binding community regulations. on the question of voluntary unpaid donations of tissues and cells, this was debated at length during the codecision procedure on the tissues and cells directive. i know it was an important issue for the european parliament and i believe that a good compromise solution was reached. article 12 of the directive states that member states shall endeavour to ensure voluntary and unpaid donations of tissues and cells. donors may receive compensation but this is strictly limited to making good expenses and inconveniences related to the donation. in that case, member states define the conditions under which compensation should be granted. both the council and parliament accepted this solution. it goes to the limit of what the commission believes to be legally acceptable, given the provisions of article 152 of the treaty. this does not mean, however, that the directive is unclear on the conditions of exchange of tissues and cells. on the contrary, article 12 conveys a very clear message as to the voluntary and unpaid character that such exchanges should have. this, of course, refers back to values that go beyond safety issues. accordingly, the commission considers that paying substantial fees to obtain human egg cells would go against the philosophy of the tissues and cells directive. it could open the door to a trade where people in need could be drawn into acts that should instead be motivated by altruistic principles. both the charter of fundamental rights of the european union and the convention on human rights and biomedicine of the council of europe prohibit turning the human body and its parts into a source of financial gain. the commission will monitor carefully the implementation of the tissues and cells directive in the current and future member states. we will do so with regard to its banning provisions and also with regard to the overall spirit of the directive, such as the principle of voluntary unpaid donation, clearly expressed in article 12. to ensure proper interpretation in the candidate countries, the commission organised a peer review in romania and bulgaria in 2004. this review analysed the situation in the two countries and identified future actions to be taken to ensure compliance with eu requirements in this field. this process led to a number of recommendations. the competent authorities of the two countries are willing to incorporate the principles of the directive into national legislation. this will be followed up this year. the commission is conscious of the importance of encouraging the voluntary and unpaid donation of tissues and cells of high quality and safety. that, in my opinion, is the best way to avoid illicit trade. in this respect, the commission also considers it very important to encourage member states to incorporate the principle of voluntary and unpaid donation into their national legislation. tissue and cell donation programmes should be founded on that basis.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioner, before we get into the heart of the matter, i would like to say in this house that it is almost a miracle that all the groups in this house have succeeded today in producing a compromise text on this matter of concern to us. i'm delighted to hear that on this international women's day. so what exactly are we talking about? the speakers before me have already mentioned this. we are talking about the existence of a clinic in bucharest, romania, which specialises in the donation of ova to european union nationals, particularly british nationals, in return for financial compensation: £150 on average. and that is the crux of the problem, because tonight, commissioner, you will hear talk about either payment or fair compensation. more than a nuance, it is in fact a philosophical opposition to this very sensitive question of the gift, of the gratuitousness of this gift. this applies to organs as well as to the two directives previously adopted by the union on the quality and safety of blood and on the quality and safety of tissues and cells respectively. so is there or is there not payment and therefore trade in eggs in the case of the romanian clinic? the british national authority, the human fertilisation and embryology authority, after a thorough investigation on the ground, responded by concluding that there was no evidence of payment. we therefore ask the commission to make this matter absolutely clear. is she, mr kyprianou, aware of any new facts in romania or in other member states of the european union? since i am here, it seems important to me that, even before the entry into force of this directive on tissues and cells, the commission should present us with a state of the art on the national legislation on gamete donation and the compensation systems provided for in the member states. on these issues - research and ethics - the alde group is clear: europe is not in a position to harmonize ethics; the present european sugar regime is unsustainable, unaffordable and market-distorting on a global scale. europe alone still spends over eur 3 billion a year on export subsidies for agricultural products, including sugar. in the ongoing wto doha negotiations, the rich countries have promised to drastically reduce these subsidies. the success of this important round of trade will depend, among other things, on the willingness to honour this promise. in the sugar reform we cannot of course ignore our commitments to the acp countries. under the sugar protocol to the cotonou agreement, they enjoy preferential treatment for their exports to europe, which they can sell at the same subsidised price as european producers. if this aid is reduced now, it will also be at the expense of the many sugar farmers in the south for whom sugar is often the only source of income. temporary compensation and transitional measures as for european sugar producers are not provided for them. it is not the intention that our sugar reform should plunge tens of thousands of farmers in the south into even greater poverty.
in writing. i voted for mrs aubert's report which encourages the development of organic food and agriculture. these practices have been evolving in europe for some years and are responding to a demand from european consumers for a healthy and varied diet. to reassure farmers and citizens, it is also essential to introduce strict controls to prevent the risk of contamination in the event of coexistence of organic crops with transgenic crops. the belgian position is similar: the assessment of controls and risks related to gmos must be based on independent scientific expertise. in addition, solutions are urgently needed to the practical difficulties faced by farmers in the organic sector: in belgium, consumer demand for organic products is not at all constant and often changes depending on food crises. farmers suffer from these fluctuations where their efforts and investments should be rewarded. it is essential that farmers are not forced to sell at a loss. because defending organic farming means fighting for quality, safety and, let us not forget, consumer choice.

in writing. eu support for research must be in line with the objectives set out in the lisbon strategy. that is why i have given my full support to this ambitious report. a country like belgium should follow the example of some of its european neighbours who have dared to invest in scientific research. finland is a model in this respect. with 3.5% of its gdp spent on research, it is already exceeding the eu's 3% target for 2010. the results in finland speak for themselves. the number of doctoral students trained each year has doubled in ten years and the country has 26000 more researchers in the same period (+50%). we must also change attitudes. on average, a young european researcher working in the us receives 2.5 times more research support than in europe. to stem the brain drain, we need to make europe more attractive to the best researchers and also to promote scientific careers among our young people. considerable progress is still needed in areas such as public health, the environment and high technology.

not reach its intended recipients and may not really meet the real needs of the population. effective monitoring and control of aid must be carried out in this country in order to reverse this unfortunate trend. it would be desirable for the cambodian government to state its good intentions regarding the effectiveness of the measures taken and implemented for its country and to provide explanations on the destination of european aid in particular.
president, ladies and gentlemen, the whole world has heard that the parliamentary elections in zimbabwe have been won by an overwhelming majority by the zanu party of president mugabe. but no one is under any illusions because everyone knows that these elections were not free and fair at all. there was intimidation and terror by mugabe's supporters, and massive election fraud. however, the southern african development community observers stated that the elections were free and that the result fully reflected the will of the people of zimbabwe. meanwhile, the country continues to sink into the abyss. ladies and gentlemen, it is urgently time for a different policy, a policy whereby the european union puts the african countries in charge. the so-called silent diplomacy of the south african president amounts to complicity with the mugabe regime. the british newspaper the times rightly observes that as long as african leaders and mbeki refuse to take action against zimbabwe, their own promises of democracy and good governance in exchange for european funds are utterly meaningless. if these countries continue to ignore mugabe, the union may no longer grant them european funds.
the commission's vice-president mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we are dealing with two closely related subjects. the first concerns the rules on maximum driving time and minimum rest periods for professional drivers. the second sets out the rules for the application of social legislation. i would remind you that the aim of both these proposals is to promote fair competition between carriers throughout the european union and to improve road safety and working conditions for the professional drivers concerned. european social harmonization in road transport is under way. our discussion today is of course linked to the european legislation on working time for road haulage workers which came into force on 23 march. this new legislation ensures that drivers operating in a country other than their own must comply with a minimum european standard. the legislation now needs to be completed. the application of harmonised social rules is essential both for the internal market and road safety and to guarantee a minimum of social rights for workers in the sector. despite joint efforts, an agreement between the european parliament and the council has not yet been reached at this stage. i can assure you that the commission is and will remain ready to play its role as facilitator and mediator between the two institutions. i would like to return to the subject itself and remind you that the rules currently in force have been in place for 20 years. of course, during this period we have seen the full opening up of the road transport sector to competition in the community, not only for international operations but also for cabotage. the european union has also experienced several waves of enlargement, the most recent of which was a year ago, and within the union, road transport has become the dominant mode of transport, with fierce competition leading operators to be tempted to ignore basic social rules in order to maintain profit levels. in a context where europe is sometimes criticised for its lack of action in the social field, we must do everything, ladies and gentlemen, to reach a conclusion on such a sensitive issue for workers in the sector. in this context, i would like to remind you of the three main objectives of the commission when it formulated its proposal on driving and rest periods for professional drivers. it is in the light of the above that i will state the commission's position on the main elements of your report, mr markov, and i want to welcome the analysis and relevance of the report you have presented. the first objective is to simplify the rules. if we try to reintroduce special provisions or a multitude of derogations and exemptions, we will make the rules very complex, but, moreover, they will be inapplicable. that is why we cannot, and i cannot personally accept all the amendments which would make this text too complex and therefore difficult to implement. we must give the citizens a clear, simple and accessible picture of europe. a simple example is the amendment to introduce the possibility of dividing the daily rest into four separate parts. quite apart from any doubts about the benefits to road safety, this would encourage the controllers to look only for the main element of daily rest and to ignore the others. so simplify it. secondly, to make the rules clearer for operators, drivers and inspectors. for example, the proposal to include all commercial vehicles below 3.5 tonnes. two questions: must every vehicle used for commercial purposes be covered by these rules? are we going to see scooters delivering pizzas and pickup trucks with tachographs? is that technically possible? i can see and understand parliament's concern to avoid the abuse of the 3.5 tonne threshold. but be careful, the rules must be relatively clear, always with a view to their proper application. finally, the rules need to be updated in the light of the development of the road transport sector. i will give two examples: the approval of the maximum speed of agricultural and forestry tractors is now harmonized at european level. this harmonization has been taken into account by your parliament. and the commission will support this amendment. on the other hand, we must recognize that public services such as postal services or gas and electricity distribution services have evolved considerably. i see no reason to exclude them. so those are the three points. i now come to the dates of the introduction of the digital tachograph. for the commission, the current deadlines of 5 may 2005 for the card issued by the member states and 5 august 2005 for the requirement to equip new vehicles with a digital tachograph should be maintained. there are no objective reasons for a further delay. the manufacturers of tachographs and automobiles have already invested heavily. all member states have committed themselves to the council's common position by the scheduled dates and are already setting up training for inspectors. if we were to postpone the deadlines any further, we would put both the member states and the industry in the most complete uncertainty. i must therefore now argue for the dates to be retained, which is in line with the need to increase legal certainty. i now come to the control. the commission aims to increase the quality and quantity of the control efforts to be provided by the member states. it has sought to develop a comprehensive system of verification of all community social legislation for the road transport sector, including the monitoring of working time. it has encouraged the promotion of coordination between control bodies, both within and between member states. it wanted to develop a harmonised approach to sanctions and a common definition of serious infringements. it is in the light of these objectives that i shall state the commission's position on the european parliament's amendments which, on the whole, seem to me to meet the commission's concerns. first, the monitoring of the application of the rules on working time. the commission can only support the reintroduction of this element in the text. the fact that you have opted for a control-based approach in business seems to me to be a logical and effective approach. secondly, the common definition of several serious offences. yes, the commission welcomes the reintroduction of this provision in the text by parliament, as it considers that a harmonised approach to control requires a common definition of what constitutes a serious infringement. thirdly, a national body to be appointed to coordinate controls. this body will be responsible for coordinating controls by the member states, both at national and international level. this provision as proposed by your parliament is clearly supported by the commission. such a body would not dictate but would reconcile the views of the various national supervisory bodies. this would be an improvement on the council's common position, which provides for a national control strategy and the designation of a national focal point for contact with other member states. then we come to the scheduled deadlines for the incremental increase in the number of checks: from 1% of days to 3% of days. while recognising the objective pursued by parliament and admitting that the council's approach lacks ambition, the commission appeals for realism and prefers to ensure that all member states are in a position to respect the number and quality of checks required by the directive. you see, since i took office i have always been concerned about the proper implementation of what we decide and it is in this spirit that we say to ourselves that perhaps it is better to be progressive, but at least we have the certainty that we will achieve our goal. finally, the traffic controls. the commission warns that the statistics to be collected from drivers by inspectors are too restrictive. this constraint could be counterproductive because it would be difficult to carry out roadside checks. that is what i wanted to add to the rapporteur's report. my services will of course forward the detailed position of the commission on each proposed amendment (commission position on parliament's amendments: see annex). i shall now listen carefully to the debate and remain at the disposal of parliament for any questions that may arise. this is an important matter and i thank all the members who are here for their willingness to contribute to this important task.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used effectively. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i would also like to thank mr markov on behalf of the epp for his cooperation and also for the open ear he has shown in the discussions, which are certainly not always easy, and in the many amendments. the aim was clear to us throughout the committee on transport and tourism: proper regulation should improve road safety while also taking into account the quality of life of drivers. the system should, however, remain controllable and also be realistic. there may have been some details on which there was disagreement. the fundamental question has also been raised as to whether a new directive is even necessary. one thing is clear: it is not possible for the market shares of those transport companies which do not comply with the minimum standards to increase. i think that was the unanimous tenor of all the groups in this house. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption by the end of the year. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the directive may be as good as it is, but on paper it will do little to save lives. the council is therefore calling for regular checks and for the penalties to be harmonized. if you can pick a little penal paradise among the various countries, you can imagine what that leads to. we are very familiar with this, especially in my area - the border region. we were faced with over 240 modifications. there's about 60 left, plus 16 more to go. the committee on transport and tourism has done a good job. i do not want to go into all the amendments, but i would like to emphasize one point: with our proposal for the digital tachograph, we wanted to create legal certainty at the date proposed here, which in our view is a little more stable than what might otherwise happen: namely that we might adopt a directive in september, october or november which should have been applied in august of the same year. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the european union's financial instruments for the period 2007-2013 is therefore a step in the right direction. that is why we must be able to discuss this date and to set a clear sign. the question of the three and a half tonnes was a topic. we believe it is about safety. but we also do not want to impede mobility in cities with the sprinters, as they are called in the vernacular. so some feel that it would be possible to work evenly under three and a half tons. moreover, it would not distort competition. flexibility was also one of our objectives, and if you look at today's proposal for daily rest breaks and also for rest arrangements, i think that is more controllable than what the council has proposed. it's a very simple number: four and a half hours of driving, three quarters of an hour of rest, which can be used at will. so that's just an example. we have tried to achieve these objectives. we have tried to be flexible and we hope that the council and the commission will also see the signs of parliament in these amendments.
president. - i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr navarro, for his excellent work and the good cooperation which has been established. the report on coastal shipping is an important report because it is a new step in our search for ways of keeping freight off the road and promoting alternative and more environmentally friendly modes of transport. if we do nothing, our roads will be muddy. short haul can play a positive and important role in an intermodal transport system if accompanying measures are taken at different levels. i believe that we should not simply build up coastal shipping everywhere in a wild way, but that we should analyse in depth which routes should be built, especially for the motorways of the sea, in order to achieve the greatest possible positive impact. a strategic environmental impact assessment of the planned routes is necessary as new port developments will occur as well as new transport flows in the hinterland. we must therefore avoid creating new or even larger congestion points. the last element is that we must also make progress on the issue of emissions at the same time. it is not that ships are totally pollution-free. in this respect, strict standards have already been imposed on road transport, but we are lagging behind in the case of marine fuels. i am therefore pleased that the hassi report, which aims to reduce significantly the sulphur content of marine fuels, especially in the most sensitive areas, is also voted on this week.

vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. five years after the lisbon european council in 2000, we are unfortunately still far from the objectives set for the european union economy at that time. growth has fallen well short of forecasts. the number of new jobs created was not sufficient to achieve the employment targets. the barroso commission has made growth and employment a priority. two months ago we presented our proposal for a revision of the lisbon strategy. the proposal had the overwhelming support of this parliament. the key elements of the review are the clear definition of priorities and the establishment of a partnership between the community and the member states for the implementation of the measures envisaged. three weeks ago the european council approved the commission's proposals. this opens the way for the implementation of our objectives. what we're presenting to you today is a first step in that implementation strategy. the proposal for integrated guidelines is a concrete step towards simplifying, increasing transparency and improving the policy feasibility of the new lisbon strategy. the economic and employment guidelines have been consolidated in a single text. they are a roadmap to more growth and jobs and lay the foundation for member states' national lisbon programmes. that 's the crucial point . we are creating a community framework within which the member states are then to find tailor-made national solutions to the problems we have defined together. a first set of guidelines calls on member states to maintain a stability-oriented macroeconomic policy. in order to ensure long-term sound public finances in almost all member states in the face of an ageing population, we must ensure budgetary discipline. we need to ensure that public spending is focused on areas that contribute to long-term growth, in particular in education, science and research. the second package of guidelines is concerned with structural reforms. they are designed to facilitate the transition to a more knowledge-intensive economy and to increase europe's attractiveness to investors and workers. i would like to draw up some particularly important structural guidelines. firstly, the internal market. we need a well-functioning internal market. this will require the removal of remaining barriers to market access and the reinforcement of competition policy. secondly, better regulation. the commission has also been active in the field of environmental protection since the early 1990s. legislation imposes costs on businesses. these costs can be particularly high for small businesses. better regulation is therefore an important building block for creating a business-friendly environment and a strong entrepreneurial culture. the third is innovation. our future in europe lies in the knowledge-intensive and innovative sectors of the economy. this is why both the public sector and the industry itself must invest more in research. the guidelines call on member states to facilitate innovation by promoting technology transfer and the creation of innovation clusters. the adoption of new technologies, such as information technology, should be encouraged. innovation in environmental technologies not only contributes to growth and employment, but also, and more importantly, to sustainable development. the guidelines for structural policy are followed, lastly, by the guidelines for employment policy, which mr špidla will discuss shortly. they aim to create jobs, increase investment in education and training, improve the adaptability of workers and businesses, and modernise social protection systems. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union. the potential of reforms can only be fully realised if they are implemented as a package whose individual parts complement each other. if all the measures are implemented, growth and employment will benefit considerably. to be able to push forward the necessary reforms, we need a strong, solid partnership based on trust. this cannot and will not be a technocratic process but must be understood as a highly political process which will only succeed if european society as a whole is behind it. the european parliament has a key role to play in this. it is now crucial that we take advantage of the political momentum created by the revision of the lisbon strategy, by the decisions of the european parliament and by the decisions of the brussels spring council. now is the time to use this momentum to take the first steps in implementation. the commission would very much welcome the european parliament's early and special attention to the guidelines presented today.
member of the commission. mr president, continuing with the remarks just made by the vice-president of the commission, mr verheugen, i would like to refer in particular to the content of the broad economic policy guidelines contained in this document adopted today by the commission. of course, the broad economic policy guidelines, an instrument based on the treaty, in line with the review of the lisbon strategy, have growth and jobs as their priority: more growth and more jobs in europe. last week i presented the spring economic forecasts drawn up by the commission services and, as you know, we believe that there will be a recovery, a revival of economic growth, throughout the year 2005, which will continue throughout 2006, and that employment will be created, but not enough. the growth must be more intensive to create the jobs needed to reduce the unemployment rate to full employment. in this connection, the macroeconomic chapter of these broad economic policy guidelines, which are part of the package adopted by the commission today, reiterates the need to establish economic conditions of stability capable of generating confidence. because consumer and economic confidence is the necessary element for increasing consumer demand, for increasing investment and for increasing domestic demand, and this is the basis which must lead us, in a sustained recovery, to more growth, more employment opportunities and, through them, to greater guarantees to preserve and deepen social cohesion and the sustainability of our model of growth and society. of course, the broad economic policy guidelines for the period 2005-2008 take account, as it should, of the revision of the stability and growth pact, based on the agreement reached by the spring european council, and in this regard recommend avoiding pro-cyclical fiscal policies and maintaining the medium-term objectives of fiscal stability throughout the economic cycle, as well as taking into account the different levels of indebtedness and the different growth potential of each of the countries when defining specifically those medium-term objectives. for the euro area, the specific need of the countries sharing the single currency to achieve these medium-term balanced budgetary objectives is underlined, with those countries being asked to pay particular attention to fiscal discipline and their budgetary situation. as vice-president verheugen has just mentioned, in the microeconomic chapter, the guidelines make special reference to the need to create the right framework to attract more investment to the internal market, to boost innovation and to increase the growth potential of our economies through increased investment, the boosting of innovation, research and development and the better functioning of markets. finally, as it should be, this macroeconomic and microeconomic strategy, which is included in the broad economic policy guidelines, is consistent with the employment guidelines which my colleague, commissioner spidla, will be presenting to you.
member of the commission. this new set of guiding principles for employment policies marks a new beginning for the european employment strategy within the revised lisbon agenda. the integrated guidelines are based on the fact that if the european union is to create more and better jobs, it needs a series of interlinked measures, not only in the labour market but also in the goods and services market and the financial market, which will make europe more attractive and open to innovation. these structural reforms must be underpinned by growth-friendly macroeconomic policies. as regards employment, no significant progress has been made recently, mainly due to slow economic growth in the context of weak demand and global imbalances. after a period of high employment growth in the late 1990s, job creation has stagnated over the past three years. only because of the greater flexibility of the labour market has the employment rate not fallen as much as it did ten years ago. at the same time, labour productivity growth has slowed, progress in improving the quality of work is limited and the economic slowdown has exacerbated the current social inclusion problems, especially for young people. long-term unemployment has started to rise again after several years of decline. so the problems we face are significant. on the one hand, europe faces an accelerated pace of economic restructuring in the context of increased economic integration both within the enlarged union and on a global scale. if europe is to succeed, it must be better able to anticipate, trigger and manage economic change. on the other hand, europe must address the problems of an ageing population. demographic change requires both the modernisation of our social protection systems and the transformation of our labour market to cope with an ageing and, soon, shrinking workforce. to preserve its prosperity, europe must accelerate both employment and productivity growth. this requires a new approach to life-cycle work and a renewed commitment to developing europe's human capital. the new set of guidelines for employment policy responds to these challenges and guides the employment policies of the member states, confirming the european objectives of full employment, higher quality and productivity of work and greater social and territorial cohesion. it highlights a number of key actions under three priorities: first, to bring more people into and keep them in employment; second, to increase the adaptability of workers and enterprises; and third, to increase investment in human capital. in implementing these guiding principles, member states must also respond to the employment recommendations addressed to them directly and make commitments reflecting the ambition and objectives of the european union. at the same time, it is essential that the open method of coordination on social inclusion and social protection, as set out in the social agenda, be fully pursued and that the relevant parts of this strategy be included in the national lisbon programmes. the european social fund may be directly involved by supporting measures at member state and regional level. the preparation of the national programmes this autumn must be an opportunity to bring together all the actors involved in the development and implementation of employment policy. in this respect, the experience of the european employment strategy has been very positive. by involving all stakeholders, member states will contribute to greater transparency, policy-making and ensure the legitimacy and effectiveness of policy measures. this is a key element of the new governance for growth and jobs brought about by the new lisbon strategy. the european parliament will play and is already playing a key role in this process, not least by giving its opinion on the guiding principles of the employment policy proposed by the commission. ladies and gentlemen, these new principles of employment policy, which are part of the integrated document that we are presenting on behalf of the commission, are, in my view, the fulfilment of that fundamental principle, the new focus of the strategy, i.e. growth and employment.
i thank the commissioners for this information. i hope that the integration of the guidelines will also lead to greater coherence. but, commissioners, lisbon was about three different aspects. there was also a social cohesion aspect, and social cohesion is more than just employment, as far as i know. it was also about reducing poverty substantially and ensuring decent pensions. how can we prevent a debate on pensions from soon becoming a debate on the financiability of pensions, since the whole social aspect has not been included here? that's my first question. my second question concerns the procedure. i have seen that the economic and financial affairs council will deal only with the economic package and the social affairs council with the employment guidelines. i think this is not a good example of integration. why do the two councils not make their statements on the whole package? finally, commissioners, i would also like to know exactly how you see the role of the european parliament. does this mean that we now have a formal right to give our opinion, including on the economic guidelines?

the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, it is probably not presumptuous to say today that tomorrow at 1 p.m. we will have adopted the final version of this directive on eco-design for energy-consuming products. the european union will thus have a new tool, a source of innovation and environmental protection, because this eco-design applied to household products or construction is the future. more than 80% of environmental pollution occurs during the manufacturing phase of the product. it is therefore essential to act at the source, where the technical possibilities for improvement are greatest. that is why parliament has welcomed this commission proposal from the outset, and for at least two reasons. firstly, the very broad scope of the framework directive. it covers a wide range of products responsible for about 40% of co2 emissions into the atmosphere. from refrigerators to boilers to computers to light fixtures to lawn mower motors and so on. all energy sources are covered and all sectors concerned. the text is also flexible, with the aim of encouraging this approach to eco-design within companies by means of voluntary agreements, but also by means of legislation where self-regulation is not possible or has failed. we therefore share the concern, commissioner, to promote eco-design with a specific aim: to improve the ecological quality of products while maintaining their quality of use. there is no point in having a quieter washing machine if it is going to consume twice as much electricity or water, for example. this is why this directive is rightly based on the integrated approach, which is to take into account the cradle-to-grave product, since at some stage in this life cycle any product generates impacts on the environment. this multi-criteria and multi-stage environmental assessment should not, however, prevent us, the legislators, from setting one or more priorities, and we have of course done so, considering that energy efficiency constitutes a priority thematic action in the fight against climate change. we are making a vital contribution to the efforts being made to meet the kyoto target. the commission estimates that we could achieve a reduction of almost 200 million tonnes of co2 per year by 2010 thanks to this directive. this transition now allows me to come to the agreement reached last monday after two marathon meetings with the luxembourg presidency, of course in full cooperation with your services. this agreement has two major advantages, the first - not negligible for the portfolios of our fellow citizens - is to avoid a costly conciliation. the second, more fundamental, is the existence of a balanced compromise which improves the council's common position on essential points. first, with regard to energy efficiency, it is envisaged that within the next 24 months, and even before the first work plan is drawn up, the commission, after consulting the consultative forum, will adopt implementing measures taking into account the product categories with the highest potential for co2 reduction, with priority for the seven product categories listed in this european plan to combat climate change. market surveillance is also a priority. since this directive is intended to extend the ce marking to energy-consuming products which comply with ecodesign requirements, this product control must be effective throughout the union. the agreement now includes a clear definition of the producer and a very clear article on the responsibilities of the importer. it also provides for control of all the commercial actors in the chain, who must be able to prove by a declaration of conformity that any product sold in europe meets the ambitious energy efficiency criteria we want to put in place. all these measures, of course, have one objective: to improve the market and protect the consumer. it is now up to the member states to act quickly and to act well in order to prosecute bad boys, free riders and defend european industry. a very brief word on comitology: i welcome the fact that the council has taken up our idea, expressed in article 14, of creating a consultative forum with powers strengthened by this draft recommendation. this will allow industry and ngos to monitor closely the implementation of implementing measures when they are needed. the second point is also to provide information to consumers and smes, two essential links in the economic chain which play a crucial role in the distribution of these eco-friendly products on the market, to inform consumers about the environmental performance of products, and to help smes financially and technically to manufacture these products which must be more eco-friendly. one last thing, mr piebalgs, before i conclude, the integration of this product life cycle approach into the business of companies is only in its infancy. so we think it will take more than one eco-design gentleman in your department to transform this essay, and we have written to you to do so. i would like to conclude, madam president, by thanking the council, and the luxembourg presidency in particular - and i think this is important. i also thank the commission for its very wise arbitration and all my fictitious rapporteurs, whose collaboration has been very valuable throughout these eighteen months of procedure, who have been able to maintain the unity of parliament to achieve this agreement, which puts on an equal footing the three pillars of sustainable development, environmental, economic and social.
it is an undeniable fact, mr president, that the recent european council was actually dominated by an external factor, namely the referendums on the european constitution which are on the agenda in various european countries. it is in the context of these consultations that we must see the decision to review the so-called services directive. it is very doubtful, moreover, whether the bulkestein directive will be revised or amended once the referenda are over. it is a fact that european decision-making is almost systematically carried out over the heads of the citizens and that in the past, more than once, old-fashioned concepts such as truth and democracy have been used without any justification. as regards the bulkstone directive, we must say that the ambiguity is a trump card. even specialists in european law differ fundamentally as to how that case can or will actually be settled in reality. it is a fact that the very broad definition of the concept of service, coupled with the principle of the country of origin, leads to a fundamental undermining of the powers of the states, and in some cases of the länder. the proposal also has undeniable implications for the powers of the states in the fields of social security, health care and education - all areas of competence in which, in my humble opinion, the principle of subsidiarity should be strictly respected. according to mr bolkestein, the service providers are subject only to the rules of the country of origin. i am not sure how such a regulation can avoid distortions of competition and even social dumping, and i wonder whether it has really served the european consumer. i am also very sceptical about the hundreds of thousands of new jobs that this directive is supposed to create in europe. i am still waiting - and everyone is waiting with me - for the millions of jobs promised to us by the same economists when the euro was introduced. i repeat, i have no confidence in the council's promise to amend the bulkestein directive cautiously. i have no confidence in the sudden conversion to the principle of 'own people first' by so many left-wing politicians who are afraid of the outcome of the referendums on the european constitution. as in the case of turkey, the european union's policy is often based on deception and outright lies, and i fear that we are seeing that again today with the bulkstone directive.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, it is astonishing how the approach of elections can sometimes change the minds of politicians and even lead them to abandon their own principles. i am not without some sadness when i look at the intriguing soap opera that is being played in europe in connection with the bolkenstein directive. until 1 may 2004, we did not hear anyone whining about the liberalisation of services, because the dutch commissioner's plans fit the european logic that service providers should also be able to operate freely in the eu. but to be on president chirac's side and not to let him suffer a humiliating defeat in the constitutional referendum on 29 may, it is now being said that the directive will be amended, if not completely rewritten, even though the socialists pascal lamy and busquin supported it at the time. the directive is therefore being suspended for the time being, in order to avoid any further advantages for the french 'no'. the left in europe is discovering that social dumping is threatening the jobs of its own people. the left is rediscovering the importance of defending national and sovereign interests. the french left, once again faithful to the traditions of the pre-war popular front, is rediscovering the reviled 'own-people-first' principle and rejecting the country-of-origin principle of the directive. whether that hypocrisy will be enough to prevent a crushing 'no' victory on 29 may is, of course, another question. voters are not stupid, contrary to what the high-ranking officials in the commission seem to think. we have a similar example in germany, where today the herald tribune reports that the social democratic chancellor schröder, afraid of the elections in north rhine-westphalia next month, wants to impose strict measures to prevent cheap labour from eastern europe from taking german jobs. who would have thought that from a former internationalist marxist? we can only congratulate him.

the common foreign and security policy, mr president, has many aspects and many subsidiary aspects and it is of course impossible to be exhaustive in this short time. two things, though. i would first and foremost like to point out a gap in the list of priorities and that is the support for europeans or people of european origin in other parts of the world. i am talking first of all about southern africa, not just south africa itself but zimbabwe as well. it is high time that the european union took strong action to put an end to attacks on farms and the systematic expropriation of european-run farms. such action would be in the interests of the entire population there. i am referring, for example, to zimbabwe, the country once called the granary of africa, which is now suffering from famine, partly and mainly because of the mismanagement of dictator mugabe. another problem that deserves more attention is nuclear disarmament. the report emphasizes compliance with the non-proliferation treaty but ignores the huge security risk posed by poorly guarded or half-abandoned nuclear facilities in former soviet states and a number of other countries, and the abuse that can be made of them by terrorist organizations such as al-qaeda. we must urgently sit down with the countries and international organizations concerned and ensure that the necessary resources are made available to defuse this ticking time bomb.
first of all, i would like to congratulate our colleague elmar brok on his excellent report, which offers a coherent, forward-looking and realistic vision of the common foreign and security policy. too often, ladies and gentlemen, we forget how young this policy really is. i remember very vividly that 20 years ago the words security, foreign policy and, last of all, defence policy were not even allowed to be mentioned in european texts or in the programmes of european political parties. ten years ago there was an embryo of a common foreign and security policy, but it was carefully encapsulated in a separate, almost exclusive intergovernmental pillar. today we have a vision and a strategy of our own - still in the making - but whose originality cannot be denied. the originality of this policy lies in the fact that we want to take care of the civil, diplomatic and military aspects of this policy. i would like to stress that the constitutional treaty contains some very important advances in this area and that this is one of the main reasons why the constitutional treaty deserves to be ratified. the annual report calls for the european parliament and national parliaments to be more closely involved in the preparation and implementation of the common foreign and security policy and for this involvement not to be limited to post-factual discussions. the quarterly exchange of views with the high representative and the commissioner for external relations, in which representatives of the national parliaments will be able to take part, provides an excellent opportunity for this. that is one aspect of the proposals which we very much welcome.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, no one in this chamber will disagree that the protection of cultural diversity and artistic expression in the eu, as well as in the rest of the world, deserves our full attention. we should all regret that these issues are not discussed more in this parliament. but, as with all social issues, in this debate too, ideology often risks taking precedence over genuine concern for that diversity. we have seen too often that vague and general terms such as cultural diversity are misinterpreted and used as euphemisms by some, for example in the debate on the place of islam in our western society, to refer to the necessary separation of church and state or the equality of men and women. it's just an example. a venerable objective such as cultural diversity can serve less noble purposes when cultural relativists interpret it differently. let us therefore define clearly what we are talking about, who the interlocutors are and above all let us ensure that the protection of cultural diversity does not lead to an ideologically inspired and top-down system of norms. artistic freedom is a thing of the past.

mr president, i should like to begin by thanking our liberal rapporteur for the quality of his report. we're used to it. it is both ambitious and balanced, and tackles the main problems: practical use and providing accurate and up-to-date information for bathers. i would like to make two observations on the almost thirty years of european bathing water legislation: firstly, we must first of all welcome the very encouraging results already achieved. i would remind you that according to the last report published by the commission in 2003, almost 96% of coastal sites and more than 91% of freshwater sites complied with the quality standards set by the directive. secondly, it is necessary to underline this observation and success and to criticise the lack of latitude left to the member states in the application of this european law. i will take a concrete example which concerns me, excuse me: i find it particularly interesting that the commission, supported by the european judges, has found that a river 10 centimetres deep, where two occasional bathers come to take a dip, justifies the designation of bathing water and therefore requires investment in sanitation of the order of eur 700 000 per area. it is no coincidence that this law has been the subject of no less than fifteen judgments of the court of justice. if we want this new directive to bring about the improvements that everyone wants, and above all to be applied on the ground, we must at all costs get rid of this somewhat restricted legal system. we want a workable law - as has been said, as the rapporteur has said - a directive which also gives us the means to inform and effectively combat all forms of pollution, most of which are becoming less and less accidental, because in the case of bathing water too, the causes of environmental pollution and their impact on the health of citizens in europe and elsewhere must be tackled.

member of the commission. mr president, i wish to thank the rapporteur, mr zappalà, for his draft recommendation, which usefully completes the council common position in some aspects and fully supports the essential objectives of the commission’s proposal. i also wish to thank the shadow rapporteurs, mrs weiler, mrs rühle and mr lambsdorff for their cooperation and constructive attitude in working towards an agreement with the council on this vital piece of single market legislation. as you all know, this directive is an important practical contribution to skills mobility. it covers a large variety of issues of great interest to the professions and also to their clients. a reasonable compromise on the text provides us with a major success story in the field of the internal market. the final result strikes a balance between the right to free movement of fully qualified professionals and the need to protect consumers. in particular, i welcome the maintenance of the five levels of qualification, which have played a central role in the operation of professional recognition at eu level over the last 15 years. this solution ensures the transparency necessary for the application of the system and keeps existing guarantees for migrants. the directive improves conditions of free movement for professionals through simpler and clearer rules, in particular for the temporary provision of cross-border services. i am happy to acknowledge that parliament has played a constructive role in achieving the right degree of flexibility for the provider of a professional service who wishes to exercise his activity elsewhere in the union. in an enlarged europe, more effective systems for managing our legislation have become an absolute necessity. the mechanisms and procedures provided for in the directive update the existing ones and should ensure its efficient implementation in the years to come. in particular, the new regime for the automatic recognition of medical specialities reconciles the need for simplification with the legitimate demands of the profession. for its part, the consultation of professional associations will provide the commission with the technical advice necessary for the proper running of the system. toine manders’ suggestion, in this respect, at last week’s trialogue was particularly welcome. it enables us to ensure a proper and effective consultation of professional organisations and feed in the results of that consultation in the decisions to be taken through comitology. professional associations will thus be closely involved in the implementation of the directive. last, but not least, i appreciate the additions to the common position suggested by parliament, such as those regarding: firstly, the possibility for professional associations to create professional cards; secondly, the possibility of further harmonisation for additional professions in the future; and, thirdly, the anti-abuse clause which will preclude so-called ‘qualifications shopping’. to conclude, i wish to pay tribute to the members of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection for their constructive approach that has enabled an agreement with the council without the need for conciliation.

in writing. at a time when the constitutional treaty requires the left to say a resounding yes, i consider the amendments to the working time directive that the cercas report has been able to impose to be a victory, given the strong pressure from the right. but this victory cannot hide the social backwardness that persists in the text voted today. in particular, 1) the opt-out is maintained for three years 2) the annualization of working time will no longer be negotiated by the social partners. it is therefore a step back from a crucial issue, that of control of flexibility. 3) periods of "unproductive" time - such as guard duty, etc. - are still counted in working time, but exceptions may make it possible to calculate them in a specific way. the constitutional treaty wants to introduce more social dialogue, but the current directive reduces it. the directive also touches on one of the fundamental achievements of the struggles of the last century: the reduction of working time and the regulation of hours per day. that is why, while welcoming the efforts of cercas and the socialist group to push back the ultra-liberalism which is the hallmark of the directive, i voted for abstention.
in writing. i abstained on the vote on the cercas report on the revision of the working time directive. the compromise text adopted cannot be regarded as a step forward for social europe either. we must remain vigilant to preserve the social acquis. the directive is based on flexibility, not on the health and safety of workers. 2) even if the opt-out which allows member states to depart from the provisions on working time is eventually abolished (3 years); this does not prevent member states who so wish from having individual contracts for three years to determine working time, thereby circumventing social guarantees in negotiated agreements. 3) the annualization of working time can also be implemented by legislation. this breaches an exclusive right to social consultation. 4) the ep defines watchtime (attendance service) as working time; however, the inactive part of the shift can be calculated in a 'specific way' (via the cao or a statutory scheme), without any guarantees for the workers concerned. in addition, the division between active and inactive watch periods is likely to spread to other sectors.
