president, commissioner, i would first of all like to testify, as elmar brok has already said, as chairman of the convention's working party on external relations. the main concern of this working party was to avoid the emergence of a dual external policy over time, one of the council and one of the commission. the working party welcomed the need for greater coherence and continuity of policy and for the possibility of devoting all the union's resources to joint action in the field of foreign policy. although a majority of the convention members felt that this could best be achieved through the community method, we were realistic enough to know that this is not feasible at the moment. a compromise was therefore worked out between those who advocate an improved status quo and those who advocate communitization. the minister for foreign affairs presides over the council of ministers, sets its agenda and is also its spokesman. it thus guarantees the coherence and continuity of the policy. as vice-president of the commission, he also bridges the gap between the community and its policies. it may also, with the agreement of the commission, use community funds to support its policies. the working group's view was that ideally it would discuss its initiatives with the commission from the outset and have the commission support them from the outset. we had even proposed that in the event of a joint initiative by the foreign minister and the commission, the council should decide by majority. that proved to be too much for most people, but i would say here that the success of the foreign minister and the influence he will be able to exert will depend to a large extent on the way he integrates and cooperates with the commission. from the outset, the working group recognised that the logistical support of the foreign minister was crucial. we have always advocated the transformation of the delegations into a unified external representation in the form of union embassies. the minister must also have a strategic service in brussels, which is made up of officials from the commission and the council, as well as diplomats posted from the member states. although this service will have to work for both the council of ministers and the commission, the working party felt that in the dynamics of things it would be best placed in the commission, even if it should be loyal to the foreign minister and the council of ministers. the creation of a new autonomous administration seems to me to be miles away from what we wanted. we wanted to abolish the pillars, whereas the new service would create a superpillar. i think that the commission's departments should be decommunised, which is also a step in the wrong direction. the commission must defend its position fully and bring this foreign affairs administration into its own services. it must not forget that it must agree and therefore hold the key. we, as a parliament, will express our clear support for these solutions in the brok report and we will support the commission, but it is up to the commission to negotiate.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. president, i would first of all like to congratulate the rapporteur on his report, which i think gives a very good overview of the current state of negotiations following the geneva wto framework agreement and on the eve of the hong kong conference. it is clear that the success of the doha round is crucial for further liberalisation of world trade. the credibility of the multilateral trading system is at stake after the failure of cancún. the success of hong kong is essential for further economic growth, but will also be a serious test for the legitimacy of the wto. i share the view of commissioner mandelson that the doha round should be seen first and foremost as a development round. trade and development must go hand in hand and greater involvement of developing countries in the framework of fair global trade is an essential part of the fight against hunger and poverty in the world. it is hopeful that last week a compromise was reached on import duties on agricultural products, and that a threatened failure of the doha round was averted. the conversion of linear import duties into common percentage rates based on the value of products is a cautious but nonetheless important step towards a comprehensive agreement on trade in agricultural products. my group, however, remains convinced that in the long run all export subsidies in agriculture must be abolished. it is unacceptable that the current eu agricultural policy costs an average european family an extra 100 euros a month and at the same time makes it difficult for developing countries to get out of poverty. the world bank recently calculated that success in this round of trade could increase global income by €385 billion a year. if africa can increase its share of world trade from 2 to no more than 3%, it will increase its annual income by 70 billion dollars. that's a lot more than it gets in development aid now. that alone is why we cannot afford to fail a second time.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i would like to focus on three points which i think are important. first of all: transparency. it has already been mentioned by other colleagues. it is clear that what is being negotiated and what will actually be decided, if the negotiations are brought to a successful conclusion, will have a significant impact on a great many people. it is therefore essential that a democratically elected parliament such as ours is kept continuously and in detail informed of the negotiations and involved in the negotiations. the constitution will give us more scope in this area, but i would urge you, commissioner, to do more in the coming months and years than you are strictly required to do to involve parliament and, by extension, the civil society in the matter. two , you 're not . world trade must also be fair. it must be of benefit to all, but particularly to the developing countries. one of the main objectives must be to eradicate poverty in the world through a new and adapted trade policy. to this end, we must first ensure that these countries receive the necessary technical support during the negotiations to further develop their negotiating capacity and also to build capacity. the result of the negotiations will also have to be what i would call 'development friendly'. that will require political courage and also concessions on our part. i am thinking, for example, of the gradual abolition of our export subsidies, for which i believe a clear timetable should be set. the third and final point concerns the liberalization of trade in services. it is important and it creates great opportunities, but we must define the terrain clearly. there are services which are best not managed by the free market, namely services of general interest. these should be kept out of the negotiations. i believe that this is not just about education and health care, but also about water, for example, which is the source of all life, as i said earlier. unfortunately, there are already examples in some developing countries where privatization of water supplies has had very negative consequences. i hope, commissioner, that you share that view.

on behalf of the alde group. madam president, i would first like to thank our rapporteur, mrs karin scheele, for the quality of this report on a subject which is of great interest to the citizens of europe. the addition of vitamins and minerals to a very wide variety of foods is more than a fad, it is a response to a reality - and i should like to make it clear - a reality of a general change in our eating habits. for example, breakfast cereals that are supposed to be enriched with iron have, in many cases, replaced meat as the main source of iron in children's diets, and this is just one example among many. that is why i think we must be flexible in this area. then yes to better labeling. no to protectionism and certain derogations such as those provided for in amendment no 39. finally, we are opposed to the establishment of a positive list for the whole range of new substances. i would like to end, but i could have started there, by asking commissioner kyprianou a question: when do you intend to implement the better regulation which is being advocated by many members of the commission, including you and commissioner verheugen? let me explain. the rigidity of legislation - fortifications, claims, food supplements, labelling directives and so on - only constrains us as legislators. technology is increasingly taking precedence over politics. so, in the interests of smes and consumers in particular, who need clear, transparent and effective legislation to believe in europe, i hope that the commission will adopt very soon the comprehensive and coherent approach we need in food legislation.
i do not know whether the average european is healthier than he used to be, but the fact that many people are part of a health trend is a fact and i do not think it is so bad. the food industry is aware of this, of course, and the food industry wants to sell. but we also see that it sometimes responds to consumer sensitivity in a very innovative and positive way. what we are seeing today, and what we have actually known for a number of years, is that the summary legislation that exists on this subject at european level is outdated and that it is therefore really time for a modified legislative framework, i.e. a framework that properly protects the consumer, that provides him with useful information, that provides sufficient legal certainty, that stimulates innovation and that really harmonises the rules of the game in the internal market. the commission proposal meets that requirement in that it provides more truth, more usefulness, a better scientific basis, more harmonization. but there are a number of adjustments that need to be made, and they have already been listed by colleagues. what we think, mr president, is that where it is a little less permissible, it is where the notification is concerned, at least the admission procedure for the granting of certain individual claims. we believe that control can be achieved by notification and that a bureaucratic admission procedure is really a step too far. we also think that the nutritional profiles proposed in the proposal for a directive to be able to carry at least one claim are not necessary either. that too is a step too far and colleagues have pointed out why. and finally, i think it is also true that we can say that it is not reasonable to exclude alcoholic products in advance of any possibility of any claim. we know that the ice didn't melt overnight. the commission has done a great deal of preparatory work on this, in the competent or representative organizations on all sides, including here in the european parliament. thirteen years ago, i was a freshman mp. my first call to the commission was to ask: where are we on the health claims? it was already being worked on. i am glad that we are finally going to vote tomorrow and i hope that we will get a satisfactory result.
madam president, 'no added sugar', 'high in iron', 'low in cholesterol' or, and this is a current bestseller, 'high in omega 3', health claims are flourishing on almost every packaging today and naturally influence the choice of consumers we are. the consumer must therefore be helped in this real jungle and that is the purpose of this excellent proposal for a regulation: first to check that the allegations are not false, that is the minimum, and then to put an end to those that are ambiguous. back to my example: rich in omega 3. in principle, this is very good, but not for products that are too fatty, too sweet or too salty. the text is full of nutritional and health claims, not advertising as we heard here today. no products will be banned, and neither will advertising. simply put, the honest thing to do with these products is to focus the advertising on pleasure, if the manufacturers want it, not health. that is why it is essential, tomorrow, to re-establish the nutritional value at the heart of the text, a criterion which is recognised by the who, particularly in the fight against the evil of this century, overweight and obesity, which affects 40% of children in europe. a criterion which will also prohibit any form of health claim for alcoholic beverages, which is the least of these things, you will admit. in conclusion, to follow up tomorrow the excellent work of our rapporteur, mrs poli bortone, and to turn the tide, is to prove that this parliament really has at heart the interests of well-meaning industrialists, i would say, and of consumers in europe.

are essential to stimulating the economy and employment. the commission, mr commissioner, says so in your social agenda, but frankly, when the lisbon strategy was presented, there was absolutely no france, are crying out for a more social face in the european union, and i think we can help to make that happen. the european commission has given a good start in its agenda, but it can be a little more concrete and a little more. i think, for example, that the commission is right to emphasize implementation and compliance with legislation, but in an enlarged union too, commissioner, we must remain ambitious in relation to social objectives. we still need new legislation. for example, parliament has been calling for a long time for a revision of the directive on the european works council or a directive on the protection of workers in the event of individual dismissal. i also believe that the european union must remain a leader in the fight against discrimination and people with disabilities are very much counting on the european union to legislate here too. the constitution sets incredibly high expectations in relation to social rights and, commissioner, please let us deliver on that now. i think that the european agenda, the social agenda, could use a lot of reinforcement and i hope for a very good interinstitutional dialogue to make the agenda that the commission has put forward even stronger.
i would like to thank mr bösch for his excellent report and for the excellent cooperation. i would like to stress two points here tonight. firstly, the responsibility of the member states. we must not forget that 80% of all european aid is managed by those member states, and we are therefore right to ask the member states to do more. this means, in particular, that additional efforts will be made to report possible irregularities and fraud. this means strengthening the systems for monitoring and managing the structural funds. there's a long way to go. this also means that extra efforts must be made to recover funds which have been over- or wrongly paid. the sum involved is a significant sum, 3 billion euros. in his speech mr bösch emphasized the whole story and it is true that the member states must be involved. they have not yet accepted their responsibility. the disappointing thing is that the member states with the highest reported irregularities are also the ones with the lowest recovery rates. secondly, the cigarette fraud. the european parliament, as mr mulder has pointed out, is the motor here. the problem was an important part of the committee's investigation of transit fraud. each container that disappears represents a loss of revenue of eur 1.5 million for the member states and the union, i.e. for the taxpayer. next wednesday we will be questioning the council and the commission about the agreement concluded with the tobacco giant philip morris. the tobacco giant will deposit $1.25 billion into a union account over the next 12 years. so i would like to scheme which is very susceptible to fraud, then you know that you have found in me a supporter. let us put the issue of export subsidies firmly on the political agenda.
vice-president of the commission. thank you for your comments. several speakers touched upon the subject of olaf. as i said, and as you all know, in july we will have a broad discussion about the status of olaf and the legal environment surrounding it. i hope we can settle some of the outstanding questions during the hearings. my desire, and that of the commission, is to calm the turbulence surrounding olaf, to develop a reliable, efficient organisation, where it is possible to follow a case from start to finish and deal with it in a reasonable timescale, following all necessary procedures and without intervening in matters of substance. we must create an institution that is a beacon of trust and which helps us to dispel the shadow of suspicion, which is everywhere in the european institutions, not only concerning olaf, but all issues. i agree with mr bösch and other speakers that we have an extensive range of rules and programmes that may give rise to the possibility of fraud, but also offer great scope for mismanagement and weak control. no-one fully understands the origins of these programmes, rules and subsidies. consequently, i ask members of parliament to support all the efforts we are now making to simplify the rules and proposals so as to make the financial system more transparent, simpler, visible and easier to follow and control. this is a major question. the commission and parliament have made proposals, but we need more concrete proposals. sometimes, someone suggests that a regulation or subsidy should be abolished because of the damage it is causing. then someone else will say that it is good enough, that we cannot change anything, that there are certain political purposes and reasons behind it, so we try instead to simplify it, with the result that little is finally achieved. i am happy to hear that there is a clear understanding about the responsibility of member states in all these matters. we are preparing certain proposals to strengthen our single audit model as part of the integrated control framework. i hope that i can soon inform members of parliament of our proposals. so far the response to these proposals has been very positive in several member states, but not in all. the sharing of responsibility between the commission and member states is crucial. mr mulder asked about the public prosecutor. the commission has been very much in favour of the idea of a public prosecutor, but many member states have not been very positive on this issue. the commission did not include it in its working programme, because it saw clearly that it would meet fierce resistance from certain member states. it was included in the ‘hague programme’, which increases judicial cooperation between member states. as far as i know, however, it is being withdrawn from that programme. we deeply regret this and will try to find other ways to increase cooperation between member states. the issues raised by mr fazakas clearly show how the lack of a proper cooperation mechanism makes it difficult to achieve effective results. in reply to his question, of course we will provide a report up to october 2005. yet this is an issue where we can clearly see that one member state initially made intensive efforts, with very good cooperation with olaf, and then the case was divided up among the member states where these operations occurred. judiciaries in the different member states examined these issues in very different ways, giving priority to different aspects. therefore, some kind of model for cooperation would be very useful. as mr lundgren pointed out, fighting fraud is a very systemic question. the commission wishes to develop a systemic response to possible fiscal mismanagement. we can never avoid bad things, but the question, in a democratic society, is how we handle such cases and how we develop a systemic response capable of dealing with all possible nasty surprises.
member of the commission. mr president, first of all i would like to thank the european parliament and in particular the committee on the environment, public health and food safety and the rapporteur, mrs brepoels, for the work they have done on this subject at first reading. if we are to make good environmental policy, then we need to have good information. the directive under discussion will therefore make a significant contribution to improving this information infrastructure base. indeed, policy-making is often hampered by the fact that information is often not available in a form that would allow it to be used. sometimes the data just doesn't exist. sometimes they do exist but are not sufficiently documented or of satisfactory quality. in other cases, information is fragmented and in a variety of different forms and types, making it impossible to use it for policy-making at european level. in other cases, the information is held by government authorities who do not wish it to be disclosed. european policy makers are either denied access to relevant information or forced to accept cumbersome and costly licensing arrangements to gain access to data. the result is that our environmental policy is not based on knowledge to the extent that this would be possible and desirable. inspire will help to remove these barriers by obliging public authorities to improve documentation and ensure the interoperability of their data systems. they'll also share the data with each other and with the public. in this way, the knowledge infrastructure will be improved, not only for environmental policy but also for other policy areas that use the same geographical data. intensive discussions have taken place over the last six months between the three institutions with a view to reaching agreement on this subject at first reading. unfortunately, this has not been possible because of continuing concerns raised by some member states in relation to intellectual property rights. i am, however, very grateful for the persistence and diligence with which parliament, and in particular the rapporteur, mrs brepoels, has tried to reach an agreement with the council. in the course of these discussions parliament has shown that it clearly understands the importance of removing the barriers i mentioned earlier in order to improve the knowledge infrastructure for environmental policy.
the commissioner has already mentioned that there is a great need for information and for better management of existing information flows. this applies to the european union in general - we also noted this in the constitutional referenda - but also to all community policies in particular. despite decades of common european environmental policy, significant barriers to the access to and use of spatial information at community level are still identified. there are already a number of instruments, i am thinking of the european environment agency, and new instruments are being developed in a number of related directives. in order to address these shortcomings, the commission has indeed put forward a proposal for the creation and operation of an infrastructure for spatial information in europe, which we have called 'inspire' for short. it will indeed be based in the first instance on the existing data infrastructures and information in the member states. i can tell you, commissioner, that we are indeed fully in favour of the aims and principles of the proposal, because inspire also aims to give citizens a better understanding of environmental policy and to make environmental policy more accountable at local and regional level. inspire also aims to promote reuse, but above all the exchange and sharing of data between public authorities. it certainly also wants to be the engine for gathering new relevant environmental information. finally, it will certainly also be a tool for making environmental policy more efficient and effective across borders. the proposal does indeed concern information necessary to monitor the state of the environment, but also to improve it. it can therefore support all policies that have a direct or indirect impact on the environment. as you have already pointed out, there has been a wide-ranging debate in our committee on the environment. i can tell you that some members wanted to go further and others thought the proposal was going a little too far, but we in parliament thought we should propose some constructive and useful changes to this rather technical proposal in order to meet some of the legitimate concerns of the current players in the field without losing sight of the final objective. i'll name a few. we have concentrated on the cost-benefit issue. you know that data providers are concerned about the revenue they want to continue to generate in order to also promote innovation and development. i think it's important that inspire itself doesn't get involved in that debate about compensation to those suppliers. this remains the responsibility of the member state. secondly, the existing european and international intellectual property rights regulations will remain in place. no reproductions are allowed, nor can the data be sold freely. thirdly, we also built in a number of safeguards for free weeding, as it is called, to avoid improper use for commercial reasons. it should also be very clear that download and processing services can be provided at cost if desired. finally, in order to respect the division of responsibilities within the member states, we also proposed some amendments, because we think it is important that the authorities which collect spatial information - and often they are regional and local authorities - should also be involved in the coordination of infrastructure and also be responsible for contacts with the european commission. i can tell you that in very constructive consultation with the shadow rapporteurs from all the political groups, we have succeeded in adopting the report unanimously in our committee. a number of compromise amendments were drawn up to ensure that parliament's position is coherent and unambiguous. finally, i would also like to thank the commission for the support we have received, not least during the informal discussions with the council, in order to reach an agreement at first reading. we have not succeeded in this, unfortunately. this is a waste of time, but we have made very important progress together. the informal discussions with coreper have already clarified and concretized a number of ideas for the future. i hope that the plenary will adopt this proposal with the same enthusiasm tomorrow. it should enable us to take another important step towards a more sustainable european environmental policy.
the rapporteur. mr president, terrorism is a multifaceted nebula that has hit europe hard and, unfortunately, it will continue to represent a major threat to our democracies if we do not reach a very broad consensus to combat it, if there is no sincere will to cooperate among us and if we do not adopt a comprehensive strategy to eradicate it. it is not enough to react, we must anticipate, go ahead of the movement. the european parliament must be able to express its opinion on the matter. i therefore welcome the five reports that are being put to the vote today because each one sets out very concrete measures to combat this scourge, and i am reassured by the conviction shared by mrs diéz gonzález, mr mayor oreja, mr lambridinis and mr borghezio and myself. i hope that these reports will be adopted, if not unanimously - which would be ideal - at least by a very large majority. the exchange of information and intelligence is a key element in preventing the threat of terrorism and effectively combating serious crime. but for this exchange of information to work well, it is urgently necessary to bring order and coherence to the regulations we know and to verify, through a serious evaluation, that they bring real added value. we must avoid safety lapses in the name of efficiency, while being careful not to impede efficiency in the name of a laxative discourse. let us be clear: when it comes to fighting terrorism, it is not the democratic struggle, waged with determination and in accordance with the law, that is dangerous, but the lack of reaction. in this respect, i think the proposal for a council decision is useful because it strengthens vertical cooperation by involving europol and eurojust and allows for analysis. the swedish proposal is useful because it allows for very rapid bilateral exchanges in the framework of horizontal cooperation between the services of the member states. in my view, the proposals are complementary. the amendments adopted in committee strengthen the effectiveness of the measures, in particular by allowing the transmission of information relating to criminal records, by encouraging the spontaneous exchange of useful information, by setting deadlines for the exchange of information, by requiring the reasons for any refusal, by providing for the submission of an annual report to parliament or by giving the court of justice the power of interpretation. in addition, for the first time, we propose a coherent set of controls establishing a level of data protection in the third pillar equivalent to that of the first pillar, including the creation of a joint supervisory authority. on this basis, we can finally explain to the police services, in a very simple and very concrete way, for example in the form of a code, what good practice is to be followed. this will help to remove the objections which are often used as a pretext for inaction. we are simply consulted for our views, but we take the initiative in making specific proposals. if the vote is largely in favour, as was the case in committee, it will be impossible for the council and the commission to turn a deaf ear, to ignore our proposals, which are, i think, balanced. we are playing a very important political role here, and i am sure that mr frattini and mr de vries will have heard us and will be our interpreters. we must also act urgently on other fundamental issues, such as the european criminal record and the fight against the financing of terrorism, because money is indispensable to this. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft budget for the financial year. ladies and gentlemen, terrorism takes many forms. the most important threat today is the violence of the nebula of terrorist groups who falsely claim the right to invoke islam. but there are other threats as well. in our fight, we must be careful to identify our various targets; we need to be mindful of the links between terrorism and major crime. we must also set up an early warning system, by strengthening cooperation between intelligence services and by ensuring better protection of the most threatened sites. there is also much to be done in terms of prevention. we must not be trapped by the alibis that terrorists use to justify the unjustifiable. but it is true that they refer to problems, which often exist and require solutions. and action on this is a breeding ground for those who are cornered in despair and open to terrorist madness. prevention also requires that we demonstrate pedagogy to show the danger of certain speeches, and requires a pedagogy of democracy to remind the importance of tolerance of debate, respect for the other. we also need to have the ability to respond appropriately when disaster strikes. in this case, solidarity must be the answer: political and practical solidarity must be shown, mobilizing all our resources and energies to assist and to repress. we must think even more about the victims of terrorism. they must be involved in the process to address their concerns, but also to show them that efforts are being made to ensure that such tragedies never happen again. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, our strength in the face of barbarism is our passion for freedom and democracy, with human rights at its heart. once again, let us be willing and unanimous, and then we can triumph over those who dream of destroying the ideals on which our europe is founded.
i would also like to thank the six rapporteurs for their report. as shadow rapporteur for my group, i have tried to make a positive contribution to the borghezio report to help combat the financing of terrorism. the interventions show that everyone agrees that a coordinated approach is needed to combat international organised terrorism and its financing. the people expect a strong european response. there is no question of the added value of european cooperation in this field. tracing and combating the financing of terrorist networks and/or attacks is not an easy task, as it often involves small amounts. in addition to the abuses through the regular financial sector, charitable organizations in particular have proved to be a favorite field for international terrorism. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. after the attacks of september 11, 2001, the world was suddenly awakened and no less than 86 cases were opened in our country. the organizations concerned all declare that they are charitable, but their sole reason for existence is to raise and divert funds for the benefit of a terrorist organization. such statistics, as well as the result of the investigation into the murder of van gogh in the netherlands, for example, show that there is an urgent need for a preventive policy based on the exchange of information, improved traceability of financial transactions and greater transparency of legal entities. the specific recommendations in this report deserve our support, both in terms of establishing common frameworks for crossborder research, developing a network for structured information exchange, improving cooperation with sustrans and developing minimum standards for verifying the identity of customers. finally, we also want to give charitable organizations the means to protect themselves better against abuse by terrorist organizations. we therefore hope that this report will be a catalyst for a structural and sustainable approach to combating the financing of terrorism in the european union.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. the first. it is indeed astonishing that the practical cooperation and the far-reaching exchange of information on the fight against terrorism between the various european member states on the one hand and between the european member states and the other countries of the free west on the other hand has been so slow and difficult to date. i am an informed eurosceptic, but at least in that area, i think, there cannot be enough cooperation. it is the concrete safety of our citizens that is at stake. we now know that the attacks of september 11 were at least partly planned in hamburg. we know that after the madrid bombing, terrorist cells have been uncovered all over europe. it is clear that terrorism is transnational and that the fight against terrorism must be transnational. the second. there can be no fear of cold water. you have to be brave enough to call a man a horse. terrorism in europe has become almost exclusively a matter of islam. american experts warn us today of the return from iraq and other hotbeds of islamic militants who have been radicalized there and who have learned terrorist techniques there. the advance of intolerant islam in europe is definitely a fertile breeding ground for terrorism. not every muslim is a terrorist, but almost every terrorist is an islamist. the third. i would also like to draw your attention to the fact that for many years now european aid to palestine has been used not only to allow mrs arafat to live a luxurious life in paris but also to finance terrorism in israel. we cannot fight terrorism in europe and finance it by vote in israel. if there is no more clarity and transparency about the use of these funds in palestine, they should be suspended.

member of the commission. the eu leads the world in the fight against climate change. it has taken a leading role in the implementation of the kyoto agreement and is equally instrumental in addressing the global debate on post-2012. however, it must be stressed that the eu cannot solve the world’s energy and climate problems alone, a fact recognised in the conclusions of the council of 22-23 march, also referred to by the honourable member, which set out the objectives for all the developed countries and not just the eu. the objective of reducing greenhouse emissions must be set in the context of sharply increasing global energy demand, which is predicted to rise by 60% by 2030, and increasing concerns over energy dependency in the geopolitically unstable regions of the world. european energy research thus has to face the threefold challenge of ensuring security of energy supply over the short, medium and long term, enhancing competitiveness and winning the battle against climate change. no single technology can provide the solution; a broad technology portfolio approach is required, embracing actions with a relatively short term perspective and others with a much longer term view. to achieve the goal of a more sustainable energy system, energy research has to strike a balance between increasing the efficiency, affordability, acceptability and security of existing technologies and sources of energy, whilst simultaneously aiming at a longer-term paradigm shift in the way europe generates and consumes energy. in this equation, nuclear technologies will continue to play a role, along with the increased uptake of renewable energy sources and other clean energy technologies such as hydrogen and fuel cells and co2 capture and storage. nuclear fission currently contributes 16% of the eu's energy supply and is, along with renewables, a major source of carbon-free electricity. moreover, key objectives of the community research programme on fission concern the safe management of nuclear waste, the operational safety of nuclear installations and radiation protection, which covers a wider field than energy, such as medical uses. fusion has the potential to make a major contribution to the realisation of the sustainable and secure energy supply for the eu within a few decades. recent technical studies suggest that an adequately funded fast-track fusion development programme, including iter, could lead to the operation, within thirty years, of a demonstration fusion power plant through which the commercial viability of fusion energy could be established. in view of the scale and technical challenge of this technology, it can only be pursued at community level, and indeed, for a major initiative such as iter, at global level. it is recalled that in the conclusions of the march meeting, the european council also invited the commission to forge ahead with iter, to start its construction before the end of 2005. however, it is clear that the commission's proposal for the seventh framework programme also puts strong emphasis on non-nuclear energy in general, with a significantly increased the budget compared to the sixth framework programme and an emphasis on renewable energy sources in particular. generally, the share of euratom in the total framework programme drops from 7.5 % in the sixth framework programme to 5.9% in the seventh framework programme. the renewables are recognised as being key technologies for europe in that they are indigenous sources of energy and can be adapted to different regional conditions. renewable energy sources for electricity generation, fuel production and for heating and cooling are all given a high profile in the commission's proposal for the seventh framework programme, within the broader portfolio approach mentioned earlier. as a comparison, the overall level of funding for non-nuclear research is more than four times as much as for fission. the potential for job creation in the renewable energy sector must be fully exploited, not only through increased research and development, but also through the adoption of policy measures at european and national levels, to facilitate their penetration into the market. currently more than 100 000 jobs have been created in europe in the wind and solar photovoltaic sectors alone. this is a good illustration of the growth potential that can be harnessed through the combination of community research effort and policy measures, such as feed-in tariff schemes and green certificates. the commission will therefore continue to provide a substantial level of support to the research and deployment efforts of renewable based energy systems. it fully expects that they will secure a larger share of the energy mix through the resulting improved technological solutions.
i am very pleased that you have given your full support to renewable energy sources. i do think it is a major turning point. i think the european union has been unilaterally opting for nuclear energy for far too long, and especially for nuclear fission, but i understood the commissioner correctly when he said that nuclear fusion would provide 16% of our electricity needs in the future. that is what has translated into the translation, which surprises me, that figure surprises me enormously. so i'd like some clarification on that. what are the prospects for nuclear fusion and in what timeframe?

in writing. building trust, not more but better europe, was one of the key messages sent to their leaders last week by a majority of citizens in france and the netherlands. and this is one of our priority tasks: to redefine our project, our very identity and to better deploy the means we want to give ourselves. we are now hearing that paradoxically the double 'no' increases the chances of an agreement between governments on the financial perspective, since the urgency of restoring confidence and strengthening the european project is obvious. and i wonder if this prospect is good news for those of us who, like myself, want an ambitious budget to make europe of tomorrow a winner. how can we hope to achieve our ambitions in research and development, the environment, employment, the protection of the less favoured regions, and enlargement if the 'club of the six' does not let go of its weight? we cannot succeed in europe with 25 people and less money than with 15! the promises have been made since the two referendums: a better europe, a europe of recovery and employment ... free promises if the budget doesn't follow. that is why i voted for the böge report, which is ambitious in terms of amounts and guidelines!
mr president, the moraes report is another typical catalogue of good sentiments which in practice usually result in the opposite of what is intended. it is always the case that racism and discrimination are spoken of without being precisely defined. it is of course problematic when they are reflected in the repressive legislation recommended here. it has happened that the largest political party in belgium was condemned partly on the basis of a text on female circumcision. according to the judge, the text was not published to improve the situation of women in islam but to stigmatize islam. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the report refers to - and i quote - 'gross racist, anti-semitic, islamophobic and homophobic statements and actions by leading politicians and government officials'. the reality, however, is that almost all anti-semitic incidents are the work of muslim immigrants. the reality is that so-called discrimination is often the result of a lack of integration among immigrants. the reality, mr president, is that the indigenous population has never been democratically consulted on whether it wants to live in a multicultural country and that needs to change urgently.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs and security, on the situation in the middle east. there was a debate in january, there was a resolution at the time, there is a euro-atlantic summit in washington at the end of june, and so on. all this is of course useful and interesting, but we are still faced with the situation where transatlantic relations are in need of improvement. this is also a matter of changing attitudes in the first place. we all agree, for example, that the fight against terrorism on the one hand and the promotion of democracy and peace in the world on the other can only be successful by strengthening our partnership with the united states and on the basis of the western values we share. but when it comes to putting the principles into practice, things can go wrong. a perfect example is the european council's intention to lift the arms embargo against china. the united states' opposition to these plans is entirely justified. it is pointless to pay lip service to democratization in the rest of the world when in practice we are doing the exact opposite by accommodating the wishes of a chinese regime which is still as dictatorial as it was in suppressing the tiananmen square uprising. we must therefore dare to put our hand in our own bosom once in a while as europeans. i have already heard references to the situation in guantanamo bay in several debates during this part-session. it is not for me to deny that there is a problem here, but the constant hammering of this issue does expose a certain hypocrisy of our own. the european union has a duty to act in the interests of the european union. president. - we can only welcome the positive outcome of the visits to europe by president bush and condoleezza rice. we must continue in this direction, but when we talk about respect, it must go in two directions.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the union and its member states lose many hundreds, if not billions of euros every year from the sale of tobacco products on the black market, because no excise duties, vat or customs duties are paid. each container of cigarettes smuggled costs the european and national governments around eur 1.5 million in lost revenue. the european parliament took the lead in the fight against this type of fraud in 1995-96 by setting up a committee of inquiry. the problems encountered in transit were identified and solutions proposed under the chairmanship of lord tomlinson and rapporteur kellett-bowman. the commission of inquiry went to the ports of antwerp and rotterdam and studied closely the fraud techniques used by criminal organizations, often of a mafia nature, to evade normal tax obligations. once again, this is a matter of mafia practices in which in recent years deaths have been caused, murders have been committed, officials have been intimidated and bribed, and in some cases politicians have played into the hands of the criminals. the excellent work of the committee of inquiry, the investigative work of our anti-fraud services uclaf and olaf, the task force on cigarette fraud, the close cooperation between customs and police services meanwhile lead to the roll-out of important networks. in november 2000, the community brought a civil action against philip morris and reynolds in the district court of new york for alleged involvement in the smuggling of cigarettes into the european union. ten member states joined the action between january and april 2001. the bare facts brought against philip morris during the court debates prompted the tobacco giant to sign a settlement with the european commission. this multiannual agreement was signed on 9 july 2004 and aims to combat smuggling and counterfeiting of cigarettes in an efficient manner. it also puts an end to the legal dispute between the union and philip morris. the agreement stipulates that philip morris will pay substantial sums of up to $1.5 billion over a period of 12 years into a bank account managed by the european commission. the agreement represents a new approach to anti-fraud policy and will allow philip morris and the european institutions to pool their resources and work more closely together. this innovative approach is likely to lead to similar agreements with the other two major tobacco giants, british american tobacco and japan tobacco, in the foreseeable future. the budgetary authority in the european union has two branches. the council of ministers and parliament. as members of parliament we are extremely concerned about the way in which the commission and the member states will deal with this unexpected but substantial increase in revenue. i am told by senior officials in the council that the bulk of the philip morris money will go into the national coffers of the member states. the commission's share was 10% and the share of the ten member states which joined the action was 90%. can the council and the commission confirm this allocation key? in this context, it seems that the member states are indeed acting like greedy shopkeepers and mediocre accountants. the 10%-90% division seems to me to be a misunderstanding of the efforts of the commission and olaf. what, ladies and gentlemen, will happen to that money? in fact, it should be used primarily for anti-fraud. i am well aware that the budgetary authority considers the so-called earmarking of certain revenue for specific activities undesirable, but it seems only fair to me that the council and the commission should make a political commitment to this parliament to use a substantial part of this revenue for the fight against fraud and counterfeiting. is the commission prepared to come forward with an action plan and a proposal for a budget line to deal with this problem? there is a tremendous amount of potential, more cross-border cooperation, more computers at the borders, more and better trained control staff, strengthening customs services, investing in tracing and tracing, better prosecution policy, extra manpower for olaf. so let us, ladies and gentlemen, let us, commission, let us, council, not only talk about the allocation of funds but also about the spending of funds. not only is it my money, but i'm looking for value for money. i also expect the commission to report, perhaps behind closed doors, on the progress of negotiations with british american tobacco and japan tobacco. to conclude, based on all this and the agreements we're making, we can talk further, otherwise not. we expect the council to come up with ideas that go beyond the accounting story and propose to use this money wisely. what is your answer, council, commission?

in writing. i voted for the excellent report by my colleague john bowis on patient mobility. the text recalls some of the obvious: health as a priority in the eu of 25 with 10% of national gdp devoted to it. health as a national policy is a major building block of social cohesion, which today faces two major challenges: ensuring access to treatment for all citizens and controlling the explosion of health budgets in most member states, as is the case in belgium. this specificity of health services justifies in my view the exclusion of health care from the so-called bolkestein services directive as provided for in paragraph 4 of the resolution. a health policy which must naturally be extended to the european level. this means that the mobility of patients must be facilitated in the same way as that of the travelling citizen, the student or the businessman. this justifies the implementation of the european health insurance card on the ground. launched in 2004 to replace the e111 form, this tool is intended to enable citizens to exercise their rights to health insurance in a simple and effective way in all circumstances.

in writing. the adoption of a statute for members of the european parliament is the wrong discussion at the wrong time. at this point, everyone is aware that the eu needs to debate its basic orientation. the decision of the french and dutch people has, among other things, been a clear indication that the eu needs to be closer to its citizens in order to restore the credibility of the european project. in this context, i cannot understand why a debate on the statute of members of the european parliament is on the agenda. the timing is more than bad. i welcome the improvement that travel expenses are reimbursed on the basis of actual costs incurred and are no longer paid in lump sums. i am of the opinion, however, that it is difficult to give parliamentarians a european status which is different from the national one. how can we explain and justify the difference in merit between a member of the european parliament and a member of a national parliament? in the light of the current events, i shall abstain from voting on the statute for members.
in writing. mr president, despite the worst possible timing for the settlement of this long-standing issue of a uniform statute for members, i voted in favour and pressed the plus button. i voted for it because i think it is an excellent statute, because it is not. the degree of uniformity is not sufficient for this. but i have learned from the many times we have voted in parliament and then had to find that the council was unable to reach an agreement that the desired community is not feasible and that there is no 'ideal' statute. the statute proposed by the legal affairs committee, which reportedly has now been approved by the council, is better than the present situation in that it provides the necessary transparency and finally brings us to the only acceptable travel expenses scheme, namely one which consists of reimbursing only the costs actually paid. i am convinced that this alone is a sufficient reason to vote in favour, even if it happens on the wrong day.

on behalf of the alde group. mr president, on behalf of the liberal and democratic group, i would like to thank professor trakatellis for his excellent work and the quality of the compromise reached with the luxembourg presidency on the prohibition and limitation of the six phthalates used as plasticizers in children's toys and childcare products, which are classified in two categories which i will not go into here. this directive concerns the daily lives of european families and puts an end to these six substances which are dangerous to health, to the environment, toxic to reproduction and in particular disruptive to the immune and hormonal systems. the scientific evidence is piling up, and i'm not going to list it all here, and all the signs turned red. it is only logical that the ministers for industry and trade of the 25 member states, on the proposal of our former dutch liberal colleague jan brinkhorst in september 2004, wanted to withdraw these six phthalates from the market. they did so on the recommendation of the scientific committee on health and environmental risks. they also did so out of pragmatism, because they know that there are many substitutes already on the market, such as citrate. the european parliament, for its part, wished to strengthen the common position for two main reasons: firstly, to improve common sense, since now with the compromise, no distinction is made between a child over three years old and a child under three years old putting their toys in their mouths; secondly, the obligation on us as elected citizens of europe to say no to these phthalates in toys, but also to regulate and gradually ban - if necessary - all phthalates. i believe that the protection of the health of our citizens deserves better than this piecemeal ban. it is difficult for me to explain to the electorate that a teething bottle coated with dehp is dangerous for newborns, as we decree, but that an infusion needle containing the same dehp is harmless for a premature baby. the danger is exactly the same. this legislation should therefore be seen as the first application of the precautionary principle to phthalates. the commission, commissioner, must act swiftly in the field of medical devices and in the field of food packaging and floor coverings. that is the right legislation that europeans are waiting for.

in writing. decabromodiphenyl ether is a brominated flame retardant familiar to this house: in february 2003 it was placed 'under high surveillance' at the same time as a report i authored, the european union banned its two 'sister' substances, penta and octabde. decabde is a bioaccumulative chemical found in breast milk for which the european scientific reference committee recommends even greater risk reduction. a substance which the european union has also committed to banning from 1 july 2006 in electrical and electronic equipment. it is therefore surprising that the european commission is proposing a return to the starting point by wanting to revert to this ban through comitology, i.e. in secret. in substance, as in form, this is unacceptable. the commission has not put forward any arguments, and it would have been quite incapable of doing so, to justify this 180-degree turn! i therefore support this resolution and ask the commission to reconsider its decision. if not, i will not hesitate, along with some of my colleagues, to take the matter to the court of justice.
in writing. by voting this afternoon to reject the council's common position, i wanted to express a clear vote against any form of patentability of software. on the contrary, i felt that software, as a work of the human mind, is sufficiently protected by copyright. i also voted, and above all, for the primacy of politics over technology, to live up to the spirit of lisbon! as a liberal, deeply attached to the values of freedom of enterprise and fair and healthy economic competition, i could not accept a text that was 'hand-made' for certain large companies, which already had a quasi-monopoly position in their respective sectors. a proposal for a directive which ultimately aims to kill the creative spirit of young computer scientists and the independence of many european smes and micro-enterprises. a text which, let us be frank, had little to do with the europe of citizenship which we all wish for. in the two years since this debate began, i have not met a single computer scientist, university researcher, individual user or head of a software-producing sme who is in favour of this directive. this could not have been a coincidence!
on behalf of the alde group. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, next monday will mark 10 years since srebrenica was taken by bosnian serb forces in the eyes of un forces, since the murder of over 8,000 muslim men and boys began, since thousands of women, children and elderly people were deported and hundreds of women were raped. the suffering caused is immense and continues to be immense because those responsible are still on the run, because not all the victims have been found, because many of the deportees have not been able to return home and, above all, because the suffering caused is not recognised by many serbs. this was shown recently when the serbian parliament rejected a resolution recognising and condemning the mass murder in srebrenica. we know from experience that reconciliation after war is possible only when the former adversaries succeed in making a common history of what happened, in asking forgiveness for the harm done and in bringing to justice those who have been guilty of genocide and crimes against humanity. the tragedy has also painfully exposed the shortcomings of the eu's foreign and security policy at the time, as well as the shortcomings of un peace-building and peacekeeping policy. some of the shortcomings have been addressed, but unity in foreign and security policy is still not guaranteed, mainly because eu governments are not fully prepared to do so. the european union and its member states have, however, managed to stabilise and improve the situation in the western balkans, both on their own and in the context of nato. these efforts must be continued and the prospect of accession to the european union must be maintained. but this will only be possible if all the authorities in the region cooperate fully with the international criminal tribunal for the former yugoslavia and bring those responsible before this tribunal.

the commission's vice-president mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to begin by thanking your rapporteur, mrs jöns, and the committee on employment and social affairs for their excellent work, and the rapporteur for the committee on women's rights and gender equality, mrs figueiredo. the future progress programme will play a decisive role in the development of social europe. in this context, it aims to bring together the four community action programmes on which the implementation of the social policy agenda up to 2006 is based, as well as a series of budget lines which relate to working conditions. the proposed approach will help to simplify the instruments, both legally and in terms of their management, and to streamline the budgetary structure. it will also enhance the clarity, visibility, consistency and homogeneity of the instruments and avoid overlaps. the commission has always been very keen to see that the revised text preserves this simplification, this rationalization. this was the case with the original proposal and the majority of the amendments which the european parliament has been so willing to suggest also go in this direction. i would be happy, mr president, to hear parliament's comments, again as the substitute for mr špidla, who was selected by the eu-china summit.
the european parliament has a duty to protect the environment. mr president, i would first like to congratulate mrs jöns on her excellent report on progress. i am also pleased with the quality of the debates we have had in committee and the very interesting compromise amendments, of which i would like to take the following specifically. i think there are some interesting changes to the commission's text, particularly as regards the role of parliament itself. it must be admitted, commissioner, that the commission's original text treated us a little too lightly in terms of evaluations for a programme which, after all, covers seven years (2007-2013). i think it is interesting to recall what the commission proposed, as regards the role of parliament, namely simply to send parliament an ex post evaluation of the whole programme, carried out by the commission one year after its completion. i think that the proposals put forward by the commission are in line with the need for parliament to intervene directly and throughout the seven-year period covered by such an important programme, they are particularly acceptable to the commission as well. secondly, mrs jöns drew attention, through compromise amendments, to the particularly important role of non-governmental organisations and social organisations. it will be recognized that, from a national and transnational point of view, these organizations play a particularly important role in the specialised networks which they have at union level. it is therefore normal that they should be involved in the design, implementation and monitoring of the programme. it is also clear that the promotion of direct exchanges of experience can lead to a better knowledge of the specificities of national realities; this is the only way in which the exchange of ideas on strategies, good practices and innovative strategic approaches will bear fruit and give evaluations the necessary scientific quality. a final word on the budgetary and financial aspects, since this is the nerve of the war. it should be remembered that the amount of eur 628 million proposed by the commission is much lower than the amount recommended in the böge report on the financial and budgetary envelope for 2007-13. i would particularly like to stress that the success of the progress programme depends essentially on the amount of money allocated to it and it is clear that the amount proposed by the commission is not acceptable in any way.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to take them up in the course of the year. but the issue papers that the commission's services have prepared in the run-up to the conference on audiovisual policy in liverpool at the end of this month have not given us much hope. firstly, i understand that a new directive should not, of course, stand in the way of the development of a european audiovisual industry. but that should not mean that measures cannot be taken against excessive movements of concentration, whether horizontal or vertical, whether or not they cross borders. the commission has been asked by parliament on several occasions to submit proposals on this subject. everyone knows that this is crucial in the light of the preservation of media pluralism. the commission seems to be taking this a little too lightly, on the grounds that this is a matter for the member states, that is to say, that it will not propose anything, and that is unacceptable to me. there is also a european observatory to monitor the market and collect data. i am sorry, but that is too weak a coffee, too much of a towel to bleed on, and, moreover, totally unnecessary if there are no accompanying enforcement measures. secondly, a word about the position of public service broadcasting. a study commissioned by parliament literally states that the independence of public broadcasting is not at all guaranteed in many member states. the commission considers that public service broadcasting policy is a matter for the member states. of course it is. but i wonder whether we - the commission's vice-president as you know, the commission presented a proposal for a directive on 15 march 2001 to combat environmental offences. the proposal provides in particular that each member state should ensure that certain activities are made criminal when they are carried out, intentionally or through gross negligence, in breach of community legislation. this includes the fraudulent transfrontier shipments of waste mentioned by the honourable member in his question. on 27 january, however, the council adopted a framework decision on the basis of title vi of the treaty on european union instead of this proposal for a directive. the text criminalizes and makes criminal penalties payable for a number of acts which are harmful to the environment, whether committed intentionally or through gross negligence. such conduct includes the illicit disposal, treatment, storage, transport, export or import of waste, including hazardous waste, which causes or is likely to cause death or serious injury to persons. member states were required to adopt the necessary measures to comply with this framework decision by 27 january 2005. the commission is currently examining the national transposition measures brought to its attention by the member states in order to submit a report to the council as required by the framework decision, enabling the council to verify, by 27 january 2006 at the latest, the extent to which the member states have taken the necessary measures to comply with the framework decision. the commission, however, has challenged the legal basis chosen by the council before the court of justice of the european communities, with the support of the european parliament, and is awaiting the judgment which will be delivered shortly, i believe on 13 september. as mr staes points out, on 26 may 2005 the advocate general delivered a very favourable opinion in favour of the commission's position, since mr dámaso ruiz-jarabo colomer suggests that the court should annul the relevant provisions of the framework decision of 27 january 2003. if the court were to follow its advocate general, a new legal instrument, this time community, would have to be adopted for the purpose of protecting the environment through criminal law, including against fraudulent transfrontier shipments of waste.
president, commissioner, i thank the commissioner for his comprehensive and correct chronology of the facts and i think this question is just in time. if the court of justice does indeed rule on 13 september, the commission will have to act quickly. i think we're dealing with a serious crime here. commissioner, you are responsible for justice, you are responsible for police affairs. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem, and i am sure that the commission will take them into account in its proposals for the future. i would therefore ask you, as commissioner, to act immediately as soon as a ruling is made and to ensure that we can create a proper legal framework here.
first of all, mr president, i should like to thank our rapporteur, mrs grossetête, for her enthusiasm and persistence in working for the health of all children in the european union. finally, research and development of paediatric medicines is getting the attention it deserves. companies that invest in such research can and should be rewarded for doing so. only this incentive will ensure that they continue to invest in new medicines for children. do not forget that they are also employing thousands of people. a fixed-term supplementary certificate of six months is preferable for a variety of reasons. however, transparency and good agreements are the key words here. we also need to be aware of the implications that a patent extension could have for all the children in the world. in belgium, for example, it was dr janssens who voluntarily made his antifungal product sporanox available to the developing countries. but now that janssens pharmaceuticals has been acquired, the question is whether the new owners will follow the same policy. in addition to the attention paid to research, the public authorities must of course continue to guarantee access to medicines. i'll give you an example. in belgium, the producer of rilatine unilaterally raised the price of a pack of pills from €2.60 to the level of the european reference price of €6.52. the added cost to the parents of a child with adhd is enormous, especially when you know that there are children who need a few boxes a day. the pharmaceutical industry can and must therefore be encouraged in the field of mr president, i would like to thank mrs grossetête for adopting the same philosophy as that which prevailed for rare diseases: mobilization against the injustice suffered by certain patients, in this case children. recently, the belgian society of pediatrics sounded the alarm: 50% of the medicines prescribed for children and almost 90% of those used in intensive care are not approved for pediatric use. clearly, this regulation is urgent, research and development is expensive and we must support the effort that will be required of the pharmaceutical companies, and therefore also support the fixed period of six months during which they will not be subject to competition from the sector. at the same time, we must encourage low-cost paediatric research, the six-year review clause, the mice programme for paediatric research and, crucially when it comes to children, measures to avoid unnecessary duplication of trials meeting this requirement. it is this delicate balance that we must safeguard tomorrow when we vote, a balance which meets the expectations of paediatricians, families and millions of young patients in europe.
mr president, there is no question that this proposal for a directive contains good points, but as other members have said, i think we are paying too high a price. i have listened to everyone, but i think that this parliament must above all be able to strike a balance between the interests of patients, the interests of the public authorities and the interests of pharmaceutical companies. the difference with the united states, mrs grossetête, is that we have a good social security system which must also be balanced. those who are calling for six months of protection here in parliament are the same people i hear most often in my own country saying that the social security budget must be kept under control, that it must be saved. but to balance that budget, everyone must do their part, including the pharmaceutical industry. the extension of six months leads, in our view, to an unbalanced additional cost. that is why i continue to support the amendments tabled by mrs corbey.

mr president, is the withdrawal from the gaza strip to be seen as the prerequisite for peace in the middle east or not? i choose to believe it even though i know that a glass half full can be considered half empty. i therefore welcome this withdrawal from gaza as a symbol that no settlement is immovable and that all settlements can be dismantled in the future. i also choose to believe that the wall, which the israeli authority has always declared to be temporary, will one day fall. i choose to believe that no extremist group will undermine this fragile hope for peace by a terrorist attack and, above all, that no authority will take advantage of an isolated criminal behaviour to exercise collective reprisals that destroy any hope for peace. the withdrawal from gaza was difficult. the israeli government has carried it out with determination and courage and the palestinian authority with great maturity. but the glass is also half empty. the continuation of settlement in the west bank is incompatible with the idea of lasting peace, as is the encirclement of east jerusalem and its israeli gnawing at it. the possible isolation of gaza, overcrowded and cut off from any possible economic development, makes it a real powder keg. that is why i insist that europe, while paying tribute to those who have successfully secured this disengagement, should not be blind to these worrying signs and continue to press for gaza to have port and air gateways and access to the west bank and egypt so as not to close itself off to its inhabitants as a trap, for international law and humanitarian law to be regularly assessed and respected in the region, for the road map to be relaunched. europe has always condemned terrorism and violence in favour of political negotiation and international law. it is not useless, even if progress is slow, to repeat tirelessly what our commitments are in the middle east.
mr president, i would like to thank minister alexander for their presence this afternoon, as well as commissioner verheugen, who i left in the plenary yesterday evening at 11.47 p.m. and who is the commissioner for the whole of this strasbourg session. more seriously now, in january, the high representative mr. solana, spoke to us about a window of opportunity, better, an avenue of hope, after a series of changes that have occurred in the region, including of course, the election of mahmoud abbas as president and his courageous talk about the demilitarization of the intifada. the follow-up has not been unfair, which has just resulted, as we know, in the disengagement of the gaza strip. whatever our views on this withdrawal, whatever our differences, and they exist on this issue, we can only congratulate the israeli officials on the way they have conducted this operation; it is a human tragedy for the families involved, a heartbreak for the soldiers and a crucial test for israeli society as a whole, which some people are dishonestly underestimating. no violence, no casualties, no civil war either, it is a success that also belongs to the palestinian authority. mahmoud abbas has spared no effort to facilitate the operations and especially to prevent any terrorist attack during the entire period of disengagement. and now, you tell me, what is to be done? it is about turning this military success into political success, catalyzing this positive energy to get back on track, because the withdrawal from gaza can only be the beginning of a process. the major challenges facing both ariel sharon and mahmoud abbas are enormous: the end of settlements, more substantial withdrawals, especially on the israeli side, and of course the disarmament of terrorist organizations for the palestinian leader. after the destruction of a building in gaza on monday and the assassination of his security adviser this morning, abu mazen just a few hours ago demanded a plan to disarm hamas in gaza. we must give him the means to continue; we all know that the battle is being fought between the palestinian authority and hamas for control of gaza, and that the 60 million euros which have just been released, the commissioner said, will be a crucial strategic support for the palestinian leader. we thank the commission, we rely on it and we will be particularly vigilant, i should like to say, to ensure that strict controls reassure the european taxpayers as to the allocation of this large sum. a time to kill, and a time to heal; a time of war, and a time of peace. not i, but the ecclesiastes. i was talking about hope earlier, not angelica. however, we are now living in a very special moment, a momentum, said mr solana, and we would like to see the parties concerned seize it with our help to move forward on this road to peace.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, the cornillet report certainly contains a lot of good points, for example when it highlights the dark side of certain forms of tourism, such as the sexual exploitation of children in certain tourist destinations or the exploitation of real estate by indigenous and often underserved populations. however, i am missing an important point in this report and that is a clear statement on tourism to countries where human rights are being seriously violated and where it would make sense for the european union to consider imposing an embargo to increase pressure on the countries concerned. an example of this was given a few months ago by mr deva, who, in connection with the paradise island group of the seychelles, noted that tourists there pay $300 a night and more for a hotel room, while the local population is forced to work as slaves for a few dollars a night for the local potentate and his court entourage. i am thinking, for example, of the communist dictatorship in cuba, to which this report refers cautiously, but where opponents disappear in prison for months and years without any trial, while the regime of an old and senile criminal against humanity indulges in the slogans of decadent western europeans. these are also sad aspects of tourism that deserve our attention.

extended to other neglected diseases. it seems that there is little awareness that african sleeping sickness, leishmaniasis and dengue fever, among others, are major threats to the health of many these neglected diseases. the pharmaceutical industry is particularly interested in profitable sales and invests only when there are insufficient incentives. tax credits, innovation awards,
