mr president, the geneva refugee convention and, indeed, the additional protocol of 1967 - we must understand this - were originally drawn up to provide for the reception of european political refugees from the communist dictatorships of the former eastern bloc. it is therefore obvious that these instruments are totally unsuitable today to provide an answer to the current asylum problems, in particular to the fact that hundreds of thousands and hundreds of thousands of economic fortune seekers from all over the world are coming to europe to seek refuge and prosperity. it is therefore good and wise that a common european refugee policy should also seek to address this problem, and i am quite happy to see a list of safe countries, even if this is only a first step and even if, in a well-understood respect for subsidiarity, it should be left to the individual member states to use additional lists. but again, this can only be a first step. the real solution to the problems will only come when we have the courage to decide that the reception of asylum seekers should take place on the continent, preferably in the region of origin of the asylum seeker himself and therefore no longer in europe. and, contrary to what has been said here, it must be in closed and very strictly controlled centres. only those who are recognised as genuine refugees after a rigorous and proper examination can be accepted in third countries, possibly also within the european union. this is the only way to drastically reduce the number of fraudulent asylum applications and to combat human trafficking. moreover, this is the only way to reconcile our citizens, whom we represent here, with the need to take in genuine asylum seekers in our own countries. because, unfortunately, the concept of asylum has rightly become synonymous with fraud and social fraud in our countries today, for which we can indeed have a human understanding but for which we must not be politically co-responsible or complicit. however, the political will and courage to pursue such a decisive and necessary policy, especially with all that it entails, such as denying development aid to countries which refuse to conclude readmission agreements for asylum fraudsters, are still lacking. the present report is unfortunately an illustration of this political reluctance, because it further weakens the council's proposals, which are good and timid, and for these reasons we regret that we cannot approve this report in the near future.
the introduction of a list of safe countries is a good step towards a common minimum policy for managing the flow of refugees to europe. i say minimum policy, because the final decision on the recognition and admission of political refugees must still be taken by the member states. it is therefore necessary that the member states should have the right and the possibility to use their own lists in addition to the european list of safe countries. the rapporteur, however, expressly refuses to accept this possibility. the report has in fact one purpose, and that is to undermine the very principle of the safe countries list, and in those circumstances it will not surprise you that i and my colleagues voted against it.
in writing. i came into contact countless times with refugees from kosovo, chechnya, afghanistan, nepal, russia, uzbekistan and kazakhstan. so i realize these suckers don't come to our continent for their pleasure. behind every parent and child is a story. a story of despair and fear. this draft directive is disturbing and the ultimate proof that the ministers of the interior and justice are playing their own games and do not want to consult the european parliament. they concluded a (bad) political agreement without consulting the ep. this is contrary to the spirit of the treaties. i therefore support amendment no 182 which reserves the right to refer a case to the court of justice to examine the legality of the proposal and its compatibility with fundamental rights. i also support all the amendments which seek to remove the concept of a safe third country from the proposal. this is unacceptable, it places the responsibility of the member states on third countries and inevitably leads to arbitrariness and political manipulation. who decides whether a country is (super) safe? is afghanistan safe after the war? are kazakhstan and uzbekistan - two countries that are crumbling under totalitarian rule - safe countries? some in belgium think so. member of the commission. thank you, because it is very nice to know where the question is coming from. the commission forwarded to the european parliament indicative information on the breakdown of cap direct payments by member state, the size of payments, and types of farms in 2000 and 2001. it can be seen from those data that there are great disparities in the size of payments for farmers in many of our member states. since aid applications are made by the farmer, defined as a person whose holding is situated within community territory and who exercises an agricultural activity, the commission does not receive data concerning land ownership or the aristocratic status of the beneficiary. in any case, since it is bound to ensure the confidentiality of these individual data, the commission is not in a position to make available or to publish the names of beneficiaries of aid from the eaggf guarantee section. the reason for the variation in distribution of the direct payments is connected with historical production. since the 1992 cap reform, the original price support policy has been replaced by a policy aiming at greater competitiveness. the shifts in policy have been achieved by the gradual reduction of the eu support prices and compensation for farmers for the consequent revenue loss in the form of direct payments. owing to the fact that those direct payments were first introduced by coupling them mainly to production factors through per hectare payments and reference yields in the arable sector, and to headage in the livestock sector, those farmers with larger farms or greater livestock numbers received more compensation in the form of direct payments. during the debates held on all the different changes to the cap in the 1992 reform and agenda 2000, and again in the discussions on cap reform in 2003, the commission actually proposed a so-called capping of the direct payments to farmers and, as far as i remember, the latest discussions on this issue back in 2003 proposed a cap of eur 300 000 for each beneficiary. at the end of the day, however, the council and the member states rejected this proposal and it is therefore not part of the current cap reform.
i should like to thank the commissioner for her reply. i understand very well that the european commission cannot provide data on beneficiaries, but i think it is important in our common interest that the member states are urged to disclose this information. it makes policy so much more transparent and the common agricultural policy so much easier to defend. secondly, i would like to tell the commissioner that, following the information provided by the uk, the netherlands has now also published the data. it also shows that the bulk of european agricultural subsidies go where they should not go, to large industrial companies such as nestlé and heineken. how long can the commission tolerate this?
member of the commission. when the figures are published, both the direct payment to the farmer and the export refunds to big companies are of course part of these figures. you are quite right that it is not in my hands to publish these figures, because they are not the commission's figures. that is the reason. of course i have noticed that certain member states have decided to publish these figures, among others the countries you named and the country i know best. we are preparing our website, the dg agriculture website, to provide a link to the different member states. that is the only way that we can put 'soft' pressure on the member states to publish these figures.

- i'm the reporter. i think, mr president, that i have also been given an extra minute and a half by a colleague who cannot be here tonight, so i think i can speak for six and a half minutes and i will start now if you will take these 10 seconds off. mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank the commission for its proposal, because i think all these railway packages are an important element of transport policy in the union and so is the proposal on passenger rights. if we want to have an open european rail market, then minimum rights for passengers are absolutely necessary to build or maintain people's confidence in the railways. i must say, commissioner, that on a number of points your proposal has been modified by parliament, or at least by the committee on transport. i should like to thank all the members who have taken part in the debate, either individually or in groups, with all the amendments they have tabled, and there have been many, and there may be more to come, but thanks to these efforts, mr commissioner, we are now at least putting forward a proposal which i feel is balanced and well-structured. there is a relationship between the scope of application and the level of demands made. the commission has chosen a small field of application, international rail transport, and a strict set of requirements. parliament has changed that balance somewhat, we have opted for a wide scope, rights for all rail passengers, as in aviation, and the strict basic requirements have been slightly modified in such a way that there is more room for manoeuvre for railway companies, for regional authorities or local authorities. first, a word about the demands. we have stayed as close as possible to the international agreements of cotif; we have amended the commission proposal on a number of points, as regards information for travellers. i do not think it is necessary to give all the information to all the travellers before and during the journey. that would lead to very heavy administrative work, tickets that are a page long and so on, i think that does not make much sense. i think that airlines should be made to give the necessary information to the right passengers. i also do not think that you should force companies to provide information on competitors or other companies if they do not want to do so. if they can be induced to cooperate, so much the better, but i do not think that a company which wants to win over a customer can be forced to include a potential competitor in its information policy. the same applies to tickets, but there must be a distinction - and we make that very clear - between public services, where you have to make very wide tickets available, and commercial, specialised services where you can, in my view, use much narrower or much fewer resources such as the internet alone, which has happened in some cases and could be cheaper. i also think that you cannot force competing airlines to sell each other tickets. you don't do that in aviation, why would you do it for the railroad? i think - and we have also tabled amendments to that effect - that we should encourage railway companies that want to cooperate and we should ensure that they set up a joint reservation system, for example, as some airlines have done. in the case of accidents, when passengers are killed or injured, we think that for faultless liability, so where the railway company has to pay in any case, the same amounts should be used as in the aviation regulation. as far as baggage is concerned, we have adopted cotif, which has a broader definition of baggage, and which also includes other amounts, but we thought it best to do so in this way. then the compensation for delay and outage. we have proposed a simpler system. the commission has made things rather complicated, but if we want to extend the scope to all passengers, we think we need a simpler system of compensation and at the same time a system not only for hourly compensation but also for people who are regularly delayed so that they can also be compensated. a person with a subscription will fall off the boat or the train and we think that is not really allowed. a difficult chapter, which many colleagues have amended, was the chapter on people with reduced mobility. people who are not seeing well, people who are not hearing well, people who are handicapped. we believe that the railway companies must do their utmost and must comply with a number of obligations. we have defined it quite strictly and have more or less followed the commission in this respect. if you've been given advance notice, there's a commitment to the outcome, you have to help the passenger. if the passenger has not made himself known in advance and he volunteers spontaneously, i still think that the railway company should do everything in its power to help the people. i think most railway companies, most railway men, take it as a matter of honour to do that in practice. we're setting up what i think is a minimum requirement. i think we should encourage the railway companies to do as much as possible for people who are less able to find their way around a station and that we can also make high demands but we cannot ask for everything. we should not ask railway companies to do impossible things, but we should encourage them, with every renovation, every renovation, every new purchase, to ensure that the new material and the new building are adapted to the needs of the present. there is a very great burden from the past, i will say, on the railway companies, but they have to be gradually brought to bear to improve it. we must have a complaints service, we must not overdo it in the language regime. i've simplified the proposal in terms of the consequential damages i've eliminated. annual quality reports, i think that's redundant. we must have a more flexible system where there is room for initiative, for wider application, for commercial initiatives by railway companies who want to do so, for regional initiatives. so we can go beyond what we are asking for, but, and i think that is essential in our position as a parliament, we believe that these rights should apply to all passengers and not just to an elite, to the 5% who travel internationally, but to everyone who takes a train. that is true for aviation, there is no reason why it should not be true for rail. we are now discussing this with the council, and as far as i know, if my information is correct, it is a difficult point in the council to make it apply to all passengers. i would like to say to the council representative that this will be one of the points for which i will fight to the last breath as rapporteur and i hope, ladies and gentlemen, that i will find you all by my side. a final sentence, mr president, i would like to say to the council as my two previous colleagues have said, this is a package, do not take it apart, treat it as you wish, but come back with a whole and bring it back to us with common positions, which we can then deal with jointly in second reading. this is a set, please keep it together.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i would like to thank him for the excellent cooperation, and i think we have succeeded in drawing up good compromise proposals on the most important aspects. it is good that the exercise we have done for air passengers is now being applied to train passengers and indeed to all train passengers, including national rail transport. our group considers it important that we have not only drawn up a general compensation scheme for longer delays of 1 hour, but also for repeated short delays, so that the companies are encouraged to work more efficiently and more punctually, and in this way we can also encourage new people to take the train. i think that's our goal after all. now, about the assistance for people with reduced mobility. we have tabled two additional amendments to that effect. firstly, disabled people should not be asked for assistance 48 mr president, the socialists have always supported the opening of negotiations with turkey with a clear view to its accession. but i would like to say to mr erdogan: frankly, you have not helped us with your unilateral statements at the signing of the ankara protocol. you know, there are three groups in this parliament, and we saw that today. there are those who do not want turkey and make it clear and who have rubbed their hands, who have even waved coffins. there is a second group: those who resign themselves to a marriage of reason, saying to themselves that after all, this is a big country, it is a crossroads for energy resources, it has water, it is a friend of the united states, it was right during the iraq war by not getting involved and, after all, there are still 71 opportunities for each country to oppose its veto during the negotiations. and there's a third group, and this third group, which i belong to, believes in this project. but as a genuine political and open project towards a europe which is tolerant, pluralistic and open to diversity. and it's not a funeral, but this huge project, which is a crazy bet that we have to convince the voters, has two aspects. the first is that we will not compromise on cyprus, the armenian question, the rights of the kurds or the rights of women. it's a marriage of love we want and, in love, who loves well, punishes well. we want this democracy, we want it very much. but there is a second aspect, which is even more difficult to deal with. will we, europe, be able to change without betraying our social model? will we be able to welcome turkey, to deepen the institutions, to vote on the constitutional treaty but also to show solidarity with this country which, for all its size, is no less poor at the moment? that's an open question. but if we're going to negotiate, we have to remember that in a marriage, there are two partners, and each of them has a responsibility.

in writing. at the moment there is a struggle in turkey between reformist families and traditionalists. the current prime minister and the foreign minister are in the first camp. they deserve all our support. the debate on the opening of negotiations has been fuelled by political opportunism in recent times, with many, as my group's chairman, mr cohn-bendit, has said, riding a wave of latent racism. the european union must not be allowed to take any more stringent measures than the heads of government agreed to in december, which is unfair and sends a negative signal to the turkish people that europe does not want turkey. those who make the recognition of cyprus a condition for negotiations forget that a swift recognition of cyprus by turkey would mean the end of kofi annan's attempts to reach a new round of negotiations on a final solution to the partition of the island. the union must support the reformers, demand the correct implementation of the customs protocol and push for further refinement of the criminal code. the opening of negotiations will move turkey further towards a secular state in which human and peoples' rights are fully and effectively respected. the religious minorities, kurds and alawites, will be better off in a turkey which is part of the union than in a turkey which turns its back on our continent.
in writing. i voted against this proposal to oblige european member states to open up all rail networks to private companies by 2012. first, a comprehensive evaluation of the experience of rail liberalisation in the various member states should be carried out. both the commission proposal and the jarzembowski report are premature and ill-founded. the proposed directive does not provide for binding agreements in the field of quality of service, safety or environmental requirements. furthermore, all the countries of the european union impose the same form of railway liberalization, while the starting point and the way the railway is used differ greatly from one country to another. it is therefore necessary to identify first which forms of railway liberalization have worked in the past and which have not. secondly, is there any european added value to the way in which member states can make their national railways more efficient, customer-oriented and environmentally friendly in the future? perhaps after an evaluation of the situation in the different member states it should be decided that this issue can best be tackled at national level, as each situation is unique. i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals, but i would like to say that i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals.
the commissioner, in order to deal with the direct consequences of the increase in oil prices, it is not enough to come up with long-term solutions alone. we expect the commission to play a coordinating and guiding role, also in the short term. the victims of the high oil prices are the people, and especially the weakest, the lowest income groups. it is up to each country to take measures to deal with these problems, but the european commission could take on the coordination of measures, for example by allowing a lower vat within its powers. the commission is also sceptical about aid to sectors affected by the high oil price, and we are thinking in particular of the transport sector, but also of the fisheries sector. here too the commission could play an important coordinating role in the transition to energy-efficient equipment. many fishermen, commissioner, do not have the financial resources to cover these costs and i would therefore ask you to increase the aid to make this switch.
president, commissioner, expensive oil is of course the best incentive for the public authorities, industry and consumers to look for alternatives and we of course fully support the initiatives you have developed over the past few weeks. in the long run all this is very good for us, but in the short term - as has already been said here this afternoon - countless families, single people, the elderly, young people, are in danger of being literally left out in the cold in the coming months because, commissioner, they simply cannot pay their heating bills. i fear that the commission, with its consistent but also hard-line approach, is not paying enough attention to this. in the meantime, various national governments are taking various measures, although in a piecemeal manner. the belgian government, for example, is going to pay back a portion of the fuel oil to ease the pain, but of course such interventions do not solve the imminent, acute heating problems of many, many people. that is why i am calling on you to do something at least in the short term - a temporary reduction in vat on domestic fuel oil, perhaps that is not such a bad idea after all. i know you do not support it, but it is a social measure, commissioner. in the slightly longer term, you might also be able to work with a kind of bandwidth, and i am not referring to a minimum rate only, but also to a maximum rate. so there is a lower limit and an upper limit within which the member states should move. we can do something about the large, ridiculous price differences between member states. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. so there is certainly room for structural action, at least in the short term. mr commissioner, and i conclude, mr president, you must of course look after the interests of the european union in the long term, but you could still make a very nice gesture in the short term, especially for all those vulnerable families who are likely to be in trouble in the coming weeks.
president. - mr machin, that was general de gaulle speaking of the united nations. today, as the 60th anniversary of the un seems to have been turned into a performance of shakespeare's much ado about nothing, we may well ask whether the french head of state would not simply repeat his once-winged words today? indeed, for weeks negotiations were held over a 35-page document which, in the end, let's call a spade a spade, is little more than a vague statement of intent. the weakness of the un is once again clearly demonstrated 60 years after its creation. the un commission on human rights, so discredited by allowing countries such as cuba, zimbabwe and sudan to be part of it, is being replaced by a human rights council. the text, however, does not say anything about the composition of this new institution or about measures to exclude such countries. while all countries rejected terrorism, they did not agree on the definition of terrorism. nor was there consensus on the principles of non-proliferation of nuclear weapons and last but not least was there a lack of necessary reform of the security council, which was again postponed. in this context, it is also truly hallucinatory, to give just one example, that japan is financing 19% of the cost of un peacekeeping operations and is not even involved in any decision-making on these matters. a more democratic answer is different, one might say.
i have been trying for some time to understand why the worrying trend in textile dumping from china has not been detected earlier. however, this development was clearly derived from the previously liberalised product categories. the measures taken on 5 september to release the quotas, and in particular the removal of textile products blocked in european ports, are, if you will excuse me, a game of panic football. the memorandum of understanding which commissioner mandelson concluded with china in june, imposing quantitative restrictions on imports of ten product categories, is clearly very badly prepared. how else can it be explained that, barely two months later, the quotas for 2005 have already been used up? now the blocked goods were released in a hurry, ignoring the june agreement and all the confusion that has been created by this for producers, traders, customers and consumers in the textile sector. i have a few very specific questions, commissioner. how do we move this forward? how can the existing uncertainty and confusion be removed in a sector which is already in such difficulty? what is the view now on the further implementation of the memorandum of understanding of june? how will the chinese imports be monitored in the coming months? and what if, in a few weeks, another suit of clothes is stuck in european ports? what about the quotas for the next few years? the latest agreement with china therefore clearly raises many more questions than it answers, and i fear, commissioner, that the commission does not have those answers either. i hope you can convince me otherwise.
the fact that we are raising the very serious difficulties faced by our companies in the face of chinese competition has nothing to do with our fear of fair competition or our possible criticism of the free market. and it is certainly true, and it must be said, that our consumers are benefiting to a large extent from price reductions and that european companies see growth opportunities in the chinese market. but the problem remains. china is a communist dictatorship with a state-run economy and the game of fair competition is not being played there. more than half of chinese industry is state-owned and receives financing without interest and without any real obligation to repay. the value of the chinese currency is kept artificially low. that and many other unreasonable factors - i am thinking of the existence of forced labour, i am thinking of the existence of inhuman working conditions - all of which lead to our markets being flooded with chinese finished products at a price at which our own companies can sometimes barely afford to buy the raw materials. again, this is not an advocacy against the free market and not an advocacy against free competition. but europe has a duty to protect its companies and to ensure fair competition, and that is unfortunately no longer the case at the moment. the commission's mismanagement of the textile dossier is what really gives us the worst fear for the future.
mr president, much has been said in this house recently about trade relations with china, and i shall therefore confine myself today to one aspect, namely counterfeiting and piracy. commissioner mandelson has admitted that this issue was removed from the agenda at the start of the latest trade talks with the european union under pressure from china. this is strange, because under the wto rules the european commission has the right to take safeguard measures in cases of unfair competition or distortion of competition. this is clearly the case with respect to intellectual property. every year in europe between 100 and 200 000 jobs are lost to the increase in counterfeiting and piracy. this summer, more than 2,500 counterfeit textiles were seized in one hour during a single operation on the belgian coast on one morning. 70% of it came from china. our creativity and innovation is almost the last trump card in the unequal battle against competition from the east. it is high time that the commission became aware of this and finally addressed the problem of illegal counterfeiting to the chinese authorities.

in writing. the three fluorinated gases involved here, hfc, pfc and sulphur hexafluoride, contribute to global warming. it is therefore important that the european union should work towards reducing their use, or even banning it under certain conditions (effective alternatives, a precise timetable). i voted for the doyle report for two reasons: first, the efforts already made by industry. the hfcs now being criticized replaced the ozone-depleting cfcs in the 1990s. this is to say that the principle of substituting safer substances for dangerous ones is already a reality! the use of these three gases in applications as varied as refrigeration and air conditioning systems, pharmaceutical aerosols for asthma treatment or fire extinguishers justifies a balanced approach between the well-being of the population and climate protection. finally, i would like to point out that we can effectively combat greenhouse gases without making a 'purely legal' sign! that is why i rejected the simple legal basis of the environment, which would have had only one effect: to split the single market into 25 different legislations. this is not the europe our companies, our employees and our citizens want!
in writing. the bird flu is on the horizon (croatia, russia, united kingdom, germany). what can we learn from a fortnight of crisis management? it is to be welcomed that the member states have taken the necessary emergency measures to avoid the risk of contagion in the union: embargoes on wild birds, the 'national poultry' label, the mass slaughter of chickens, the quarantine of species... however, it is hard not to deplore the lack of coordination between the 25 countries, each playing its own "health" part, when it was to deal with all these risks of pandemics that europe set up a european centre for disease prevention and control in 2003, following the sars crisis. stockholm should be the centre of coordination and health surveillance in europe. it's not! until the member states are willing to give the europe of health a chance, it is essential that we prepare ourselves for the annual migration of wild birds next spring. let us hope that europe will use these six months to develop a vaccine and ensure that the conditions are not right for avian influenza to emerge in the union.

member of the commission. ladies and gentlemen, this debate has shown how complex and multifaceted the problem of globalisation is, and how difficult it is to find clear solutions. i think it also made clear what we suspected after all, that globalization is a fairly old story and has been going on for at least 300 to 400 years. it is also clear that globalization will continue, that the interconnectedness of the world and its close contacts are part of our lives, and that it is therefore very necessary to give globalization a human face, to give it a social dimension. it is also clear that no simple solution can be found, that no initiative can be found that is capable of solving the problem in its entirety. i think it is very important that the european union has decided to pay attention to the social dimension, to work on this social dimension in close cooperation with the united nations and, in particular, with the international labour organisation. it is a path that is capable of producing results, in some cases perhaps partial, but nevertheless i think it is very right that we have embarked on it. i listened to this debate with great interest and i will also follow with great interest the development of the report that has been prepared and the structure of the amendments adopted.
member of the commission. ladies and gentlemen, the commission has committed itself to speeding up the process of transition to digital broadcasting at european union level and to designing this process in such a way as not to distort competition. the aim is to achieve the analogue switch-off by the beginning of 2012 at the latest and to ensure that the spectrum freed up by the analogue switch-off is used in a manner that maximises the benefits for society and that society can start using it for hybrid television, telecommunications or non-broadcasting services. the transition to digital broadcasting in the european union is accelerating. the deadlines for switching off analogue terrestrial television broadcasting have recently been reduced by 2 years in spain from 2012 to 2010 and by 3 years in germany from 2010 to 2007. ten member states have set the shutdown date for 2010 or earlier, another ten have set it for 2012, five member states and bulgaria and romania have not yet decided on the shutdown date. poland, ireland and bulgaria meanwhile indicate that they may have difficulties in meeting the 2012 deadline. on this basis, the commission expects that by early 2010 the transition process should be well advanced in the european union as a whole and that the deadline for switching off analogue terrestrial television in 2012 will be met in the vast majority of member states. as regards protection against interference from analogue systems located outside europe, the commission supports the idea of agreeing at international level to end legal protection for the use of analogue transmission spectrum by 2015. this rapid termination of legal protection should encourage non-eu countries to either switch off analogue systems or at least reopen negotiations with neighbouring eu member states to minimise interference with digital systems. the international approach to scheduling should not exclude the introduction of new broadcasting and non-broadcasting services in the same bands in the future. from the outset, the commission calls for maximum technical flexibility and for consideration to be given to making part of the spectrum dividend available on a pan-european basis for future innovation services. europe fully supports research and development through its ist framework programmes for research into new enabling technologies that will enable the deployment of convergence services. as an example of newly introduced services, the community has financed several r & d projects related to interactive television, which is the basis of the electronic state news services offered to italian citizens. digital radio was also developed by a consortium set up under a european union research programme. digital radio can now broadcast in all analogue bands of the spectrum. in the same spectrum, which was previously used for a single analogue channel, several digital channels can be broadcast in hifi quality. allow me to make a brief comment on the motion for a resolution. the commission welcomes the support of the european parliament in speeding up the transition and in setting the beginning of 2012 as the deadline for completing the process of switching off analogue broadcasting in all member states, as proposed by the commission. in view of the fact that you are adopting this resolution today, i cannot answer all the detailed questions that this extensive document addresses directly at this time. the european commission will carefully consider the scope of the policy orientation for new and innovative services to be operated in originally analogue spectrum bands. at the moment, most of the most innovative services in this field are more the subject of research and development supported by the commission's research programmes. in the long term, considerable experience is needed with the technical and commercial viability of these services. spectrum issues will be a key element of the commission's work in this area. these issues will also be the subject of intensive discussions with member states in the ongoing work of the communications committee and its radio and television sub-group, as well as in the radio spectrum committee and the radio spectrum policy group. the regulatory framework addresses the issues of vertical integration and significant market power. the wholesale market for radio and television broadcast signal transmissions is included in the list of relevant markets and significant market power issues are addressed through appropriate regulatory intervention. the commission is working on a communication on the interoperability of interactive television and plans to adopt it by the end of this year. in its decisions on state aid cases, the commission provides guidance on the aspects of financing the switch-over to digital broadcasting. the european union legislation provides for a clear separation of the regulation of the transmission of electronic signals from the regulation of content. the commission considers the support of the european parliament for maintaining this clear separation to be very useful.
i would like to ask you, mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, to pay attention in this short space of time to two aspects of this digital changeover, starting with open standards, to which mrs barsi-pataky has of course already referred. the commission itself decided in march, commissioner, that national governments could provide subsidies for pilot projects, for example, but also for the purchase of set-top boxes by individual consumers. i wonder if you should not specify that, tighten it up, in the sense that you should stipulate that such financial support, subsidy, is only possible for set-top boxes with a so-called open standard and to avoid two things. in order to avoid the consumer being charged twice and, on the other hand, to avoid any industrial partner deliberately setting up so-called bottlenecks, the commission has proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a proposal for a directive on the protection of consumers. because, commissioner, if one thing is clear, it is that the new television viewing, the digital tv viewing, is becoming more expensive for consumers; so it's crucial that we also agree on a basic set of channels that are accessible to everyone and that remain so. i assume you agree with us that in future the providers should be obliged to offer their customers, in particular, the basic channels of public service broadcasting; the so-called 'must-carry' is not an obstacle to the digital switchover, on the contrary, it is in our view a guarantee for maximum dissemination. then my second point. pluralism and diversity; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the internal market. that is why in the motion for a resolution which we adopted in the committee on industry we expressly ask you to ensure that the new digital market does not come under the exclusive control of some multinational. the text reads: 'a majority or a relevant part of the new broadcasting capacity or of the audiovisual companies must not come under the exclusive control of a media multinational'. that, commissioner, must become one of the priorities, one of your priorities in this whole process of change. i do not need to refer to the link with the directive on television without frontiers, which will be on our table in the first place, before the end of this year or the beginning of next year. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the european commission, mr špidla, must take the side of the consumer in this debate. i hope very much that you will not use the subsidiarity argument too easily to avoid your responsibility, because i think that argument is being used in a completely wrong way here.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0176/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 4064/89 on the common organization of the market in fruit and vegetables (com (97) 0406 - c4-0177/94 - 00/0425 (cns)) a first cursory reading of the annual report shows that the commission has become better organised since the fall of the santer commission and the start of the reform process. the good news is that the european budget's rate of spending has improved for the third year in a row. the bad news is that for the eleventh year in a row the court of auditors has not been able to deliver a positive statement of assurance, a positive das. so there is no certainty about the reliability of the figures. the reason is clear: the monitoring and control systems are either not yet in place, are not working effectively or payments are materially flawed. ladies and gentlemen, we are talking here about agricultural and structural policies, internal and external policies, more than four-fifths of the total european budget of 105 billion. the central problem is that 80 to 85% of the budget is shared with the member states for management and control. the finance ministers are refusing to take responsibility for this and that is a disgrace. they want money from brussels, but not the accountability. the annual report shows us once again the vital problem of export refunds. this is equivalent to 3.6 billion euros or 7.5% of the agricultural budget. however, in value terms, they account for 26% of the irregularities reported to the commission. the member states should physically check 5% of export refund files, but they are also failing miserably. i welcome the recommendation in paragraph 4.30 and footnote 20 of the annual report that the checks should be carried out at the level of the final beneficiaries. and i quote: "such a scope would create a stronger and clearer chain of accountability for cap spending". i therefore warmly urge you once again to make the lists of the final beneficiaries of agricultural payments public. this has already happened in estonia, denmark, the united kingdom and the netherlands. flanders and belgium, on the other hand, created a false openness by lumping all the figures together, making a sound analysis impossible. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. for example, in the netherlands, tobacco giant philip morris received 6.5 million euros for adding sugar to cigarettes over the past five years. in view of the high sugar price at eu level, philip morris is compensated for this. the klm catering service received eur 646 000 because it also uses sugar, dairy products, vegetables and fruit on flights outside the european union. this is considered as exports and can therefore be considered as export refund aid. this is crazy. this is just the tip of the iceberg. europe, the european union, the european agricultural policy will only be acceptable to the public if these waste products are dealt with. i wish all the rapporteurs in the discharge procedure, mr mulder in particular, good luck and i hope that in the coming months we will be able to carry out our work in a good way and to deliver good discharge reports at the april partsession next year.
for the greens/efa group mr president, mr president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen, i am very pleased to see that the commission has taken the initiative in this area, and i am very pleased to see that the commission has taken the initiative in this area. to hear you say "prosperity, solidarity, security", that sounds good. there are other triptychs: freedom, equality, brotherhood. the charter of fundamental rights also contains the word dignity, or justice. but there is one concept missing from the call: sustainability. i thought to myself, actually, in english, sustainable, it sounds good. in french, sustainability already sounds less good. and then sustainability, as we say, sounds even worse. maybe another triptych could be: live, move and love. just a suggestion! seriously, what's the emergency? member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the eu action plan called flegt - forest regulation, governance and trade - is concerned only with the concept of legality and does not address the wider and more complex issues of sustainable development of forestry. there is a marked difference between these two aspects. wood of legal origin may be harvested in an environmentally harmful way, for example by means of authorised clearing. similarly, illegal timber may come from a sustainable source, such as timber harvested under indigenous management systems that, while environmentally friendly, are not subject to official legal requirements. however, in most countries, forestry legislation is based on sustainable development principles. improved governance and better enforcement will therefore lead to more environmentally friendly forest management. the partnership agreements to be concluded under the flegt action plan will also provide a means of bringing together stakeholders to address broader aspects of forest governance and, where appropriate, support the implementation of regulatory legislative reforms. the commission hopes that these procedures will improve legislation and governance in the partner states and make them more equitable. therefore, i hope you will understand that the importance given in the flegt action plan to the issue of legality is not an end in itself but rather a means of working with partner states to improve governance in the forest sector. however, the eu flegt action plan is an initiative that aims to bring about change through the commitment of the parties. it is therefore not desirable to impose strict social and environmental conditions on potential partner states. too stringent conditions would be an obstacle to their commitment to the partnership agreements. it should also be noted that, while the eu's flegt action plan focuses on governance and legality, the eu remains committed to promoting sustainable forest management in developing countries. over the past decade, the european union has allocated more than €700 million to sustainable forest management in asia, central africa and south america.

sectors currently in the hands of the generals. it would be interesting to invite the commercial and industrial companies in burma to set an example, so that the country can really move towards democracy. that is why i, of course, wholeheartedly support the resolution of the six political groups.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. because as commissioner mandelson has said: it's clear we need to adjust our ambitions. it is clear that hong kong will not lead to the long-awaited breakthrough in multilateral trade negotiations, unless a miracle happens. hopefully the meaning of hong kong will not be changed from "smelly harbour" to "sand mud puddle". the biggest obstacle is and remains the agricultural dossier. europe does not want to make concessions if other large countries do not come up with more concessions. and so we are in a complete deadlock. the situation seems even worse with the gats negotiations, where there is no agreement at all. i believe the commissioner is right when he says that a doha round without a substantive and positive approach to the services sector is unacceptable for europe. so we're supposed to put our hands in our own bosom? i don't think he is. europe has gone far, very far indeed, in the multilateral trade negotiations. we already have the largest open economy in the world. the european scheme of generalised preferences is the most generous preferential system. and everything but arms is unique in terms of exports to europe by the least developed countries. but at the moment the very principle of multilateral trade negotiations is at stake. a new debacle in hong kong risks completely eroding the world trade organization, as trading blocs will seek and pursue alternatives such as bilateral and regional trade agreements to increase their market access. this scenario is best avoided. on the one hand, because the poorest countries are in danger of being the victims, but on the other hand, because the importance of the world trade organisation, which has contributed to more economic growth, more development and more jobs over the last 50 years, cannot and must not be underestimated. the european union is a democratic institution, but it is also a democratic institution, even though it is in dire need of reform, in the direction of greater transparency, greater efficiency and, above all, more democratic control. commissioner, with the resolution before us, which we shall be voting on tomorrow, we want to strengthen your negotiating mandate, your negotiating position. the fact that there is a relatively large consensus in europe hopefully makes your mission a little less impossible. whatever happens, we must remain the driving force behind the multilateral trade negotiations from europe. your most important job now is to make sure there's life after hong kong. we shall endeavour to support you in this endeavour, modestly but with great conviction.
mr president, sport is much more than just a game. it is indeed a dream instrument with which to achieve very important things in education and in the social field. the arguments in the motion for a resolution are of course equally valid for sport here in europe. sport promotes common values and should therefore not be treated as a purely economic sector. i know, and you know with us, that there is a lot of money involved in sport and that football clubs are often powerful companies that are run as such. and that should not be an argument for remaining blind to the enormous social role of sports organisations. many clubs, big and small, in the european football league are active in the social field, in community projects. they involve young people from socially weaker families, often of immigrant origin or young people with learning difficulties, in the club and they thus avoid the young people slipping away and becoming lost to society. we must cherish and encourage this function of sport. projects involving local authorities and football clubs working closely together to achieve what is known as greater social cohesion deserve support. and just as we are debating this, there is a hearing being held in parliament, not far from here, on football as an instrument against racism. well, you see. i hope very much, commissioner and mr president-in-office, that you will be with us in the coming months and years in the development of european sport policy with or without a constitution. a sport policy which pays particular attention to the undeniable social role of sport in the european union and beyond.
the rapporteur. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, as the commissioner has said, my report is about implementing a decision taken by the general council of the world trade organisation on 30 august 2003. this decision allows wto members, pursuant to article 6 of the doha declaration on trips, to grant compulsory licences for the manufacture and sale of patented pharmaceutical products to countries with an acute public health problem which have insufficient or no production capacity of their own. it is almost the only agreement that was reached at that time in cancún among the wto members. the aim of my report is to ensure that this wto decision is applied in a uniform and equal manner in all eu member states by means of a european regulation. the commission's proposal is not a new one, but it is a new one. this consensus put us in a particularly strong negotiating position in the informal trialogue which was immediately launched. the british presidency in particular, and i do not blame them for their absence this evening, was keen to see an agreement at first reading with a view to the wto ministerial conference in hong kong. almost all the proposals of the committee on international trade were eventually accepted. this is thanks to the excellent cooperation with the shadow rapporteurs of the other political parties who have defended parliament's positions with a great deal of knowledge and conviction, for which i am very grateful to them. the main improvement on the commission proposal is undoubtedly the extension of the scope to non-wto members. all developing countries will now be able to use the system if necessary. in addition, ngos and international organisations such as the un will have the right to submit applications for the import of medicines themselves. in fact, these organizations often distribute medicines in developing countries. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the application of the system of quotas for the period from 1 january 1993 to 31 december 1993. an application is possible if it appears that the quantity of medicinal products initially granted in the compulsory licence is not sufficient to alleviate the need. another important change is the removal of the requirement to negotiate with the patent holder in advance in the event of a major emergency or other situations of non-commercial or public use. as regards these preliminary negotiations, the committee on international trade has insisted on a more precise period of up to 30 days. this was eventually accepted by the council, as was the specific definition of the concept of appropriate compensation to be paid to the rightholder. it now simply represents 4% of the total value of the product concerned in case of an emergency. in other cases, the economic value is taken into account, but 4% remains the criterion. the amendments which the compromise contains to the commission's initial proposal were incorporated in amendments which i tabled together with the shadow rapporteurs of the other groups. i hope that they will be adopted and that the agreement reached with the council and the commission will be confirmed. it will enable us to announce in hong kong that europe has translated this important wto decision into a uniform regulation for the whole union. this can only serve to enhance our credibility in relation to the many people in need in developing countries and in regions affected by natural disasters. it is in that spirit that i would urge my colleagues to accept the amendments and thus ratify the agreement reached.
member of the commission. mr president, before i start my introductory remarks i would like to offer my apologies to you and to the honourable members for the delay. we had some traffic problems but now i am here and i will let you know the position of the commission. i would like to express my thanks to the rapporteur, mr becsey, for his two reports, and to ms berès for the oral question on such an important issue which is very close to my heart. i will first comment on the proposal on the minimum standard vat rate. i will continue with comments on the oral question and the motion for a resolution concerning the reduced vat rate. finally, i will comment briefly on the eighth vat directive proposal, which is part of the one-stop-shop package on which an extended debate took place in this house back in september. the commission thanks the european parliament for the quick handling of the minimum standard vat rate proposal and in particular the rapporteur, mr becsey, for his active and positive contribution. this proposal needs to be adopted as a matter of urgency as the present rules come to an end on 31 december 2005. the absence of a timely decision would imply a legal gap in eu legislation. the rules concerning the standard vat rate currently in force combined with the transitional system mechanisms have functioned so far to an acceptable degree. it is therefore appropriate to maintain the current minimum standard rate at 15% for a further period of five years in order to cover the ongoing implementation of the strategy to simplify and modernise the current community legislation on vat. for your information, the proposal has received a positive reaction from the european economic and social committee. the committee has acknowledged that in the current environment – i.e. the rule of unanimity – the commission’s proposal is the best way forward. i would like to stress that, while i fully understand the concerns of the honourable members regarding the absence of a decision in the council only one month away from the expiry date for the reduced rates for labour-intensive services, i do not think that the current proposal can be used to solve this problem. therefore i would very much urge honourable members not to make a link between this dossier and the discussions on the scope of the reduced vat rates which are currently being held in the council on the basis of the commission’s 2003 compromise proposal. i now come to the oral question and the motion for a resolution concerning the reduced vat rates on labour-intensive services. i would like to thank you for putting this question to the commission. i am conscious that this issue is of high concern to the public and to business. i am also very much concerned about this issue as the current regime to apply reduced vat rates on labour-intensive services is due to expire at the end of this year. i very much appreciate the uk presidency’s efforts to find a compromise on reduced vat rates. this issue was discussed at the ecofin council on 8 november and will be discussed again on 6 december. the absence of a decision by the council before the end of this year on the application of reduced rates to labour-intensive services would be a very bad outcome. businesses are very concerned as they need legal certainty. i can assure you that the commission will do its utmost to help the presidency reach a conclusion on this matter. without going into details about the presidency’s compromise, i want to explain the difficulties that have arisen in previous debates on this issue. some delegations reject any extension of annex h as a matter of principle. i must say that i do not really understand that position. the reduced rates listed in annex h are optional, so that, apart from a few categories in respect of which reduced rates are largely applied throughout europe, there are already today huge disparities between member states. if we take into account the numerous specific derogations granted to some member states, these disparities become even bigger. restaurant services are a very good example. currently half of the member states apply reduced rates to restaurant services by way of specific derogations and this has not given rise to any complaints or distortion of competition. why not, therefore, give the opportunity to all member states to apply a reduced rate in this sector if they wish to do so? some member states press for more subsidiarity. the budgetary impact of applying additional reduced rates also comes into play. in my view only very few sectors are really relevant in this respect. in principle, only restaurants and construction services might have a significant potential budgetary impact. for instance, regarding the building sector the council could explore criteria for restricting the scope of the reduced rates in order to limit the possible budgetary impact for example by limiting the reduced rate to buildings over a certain age. i want to underline the economic importance of the building sector. you know that the commission has always been doubtful about the positive employment effects of introducing reduced vat rates. this is still the case. nevertheless, we have to realise that at least in this sector the vat rate plays an important role. in contrast to the reduction of the rate, its increase by the reintroduction of the standard rate could well lead to an immediate increase in prices for consumers. this would have negative consequences for employment and could even encourage the black economy in the member states concerned. i am aware that businesses, the member states concerned and the european parliament have great concerns in this regard. therefore i consider that there are good political and economic arguments to allow at least the preservation of the status quo in this sector. however, there are some conditions. first, only locally delivered services should be added to the existing scope of the reduced vat rate provisions. the extension of reduced rates to new categories of goods should be avoided, as the risk of creating distortions of competition is too high. second, the mere prolongation of the existing experimental reduced rates for labour-intensive services is neither viable nor desirable. although i was initially tempted by this solution, we have to recognise that it is an illusion to believe that a mere prolongation has more chance of being unanimously adopted by the council than the compromise currently under discussion. furthermore, this experiment has been limited to nine member states and a mere prolongation would only prolong the unequal treatment of member states. i have to stress that the council has already had more than two years to reach a decision. it should not duck its responsibilities yet again. third, the compromise must be fair in the sense that it should represent a step towards the equal treatment of all member states. in my view, the uk presidency proposal for an initial extension of the time-limited derogations for the new member states to 2015 would precisely contribute to this objective. i know that some of the honourable members might not entirely agree with this line, especially as regards the extension of the labour-intensive services. let me, therefore, take this opportunity to remind them of the fact that when the council agreed on the second extension of the reduced rates for labour-intensive services for two more years, the commission considered that the same possibilities should be offered to the new member states. at the time of the pre-accession negotiations, the possibility of applying reduced rates to labour-intensive services was not considered relevant since the experiment was due to end on 31 december 2003, i.e. before eu enlargement. therefore, the extension of measures constituted a new element calling for a review of the situation. the situation is clearly not the same as it was in 2003 and it is therefore exceedingly unlikely that the council will – as it did previously – unanimously request that the commission propose an extension. in addition, a simple extension of the labour-intensive services experiment offers no solutions for the widespread demand to also include restaurant services in the scope of the reduced rates, because currently they are not in annex k. in conclusion, i consider it to be imperative that the council should now draw up the lines of a viable compromise with a view to a political decision in december during the last ecofin meeting under the uk presidency. however, the commission is in the hands of the council and we can do no more than help the council as much as possible to reach an agreement. as the commissioner responsible for taxation, i can assure you that the commission is prepared to do its utmost and show the maximum flexibility during the ecofin meeting on 6 december in order to help reach unanimous agreement on the basis of the uk compromise. finally, i should like to comment briefly on the eighth directive proposal. on several occasions i have underlined that one of the ways of attaining the lisbon objectives is to reduce cross-border administrative burdens and compliance costs in order to facilitate community-wide activities by businesses. today it will be possible to take the next step towards this objective. the proposal which is tabled today is part of the larger package known as the one-stop-shop scheme. the other parts of the overall proposal – the simplification measures, whereby taxable persons can discharge their vat obligations in the member states of establishment, and the rules containing the exchange of information between member states – were adopted by this parliament in september. this part of the proposal aims at reforming the vat refund procedure for traders who have to reclaim their vat in a member state in which they are not established. an entirely electronic procedure will replace the current burdensome paper-based eighth directive refund system. as a result of the new proposal, it will become possible to speed up the vat refund procedure substantially. this will be of particular help for small and medium-sized enterprises. in this respect i welcome the positive contribution of the rapporteur, although i cannot accept the proposed amendments. most of the issues are actually already covered by the commission’s proposal on the one-stop-shop package. i therefore invite you to support the original position of the commission. now i am eager to listen to your opinion on the three vat dossiers on the agenda of this meeting and in my closing remarks i shall take a position on the amendments that have been tabled.

i would like to begin by thanking mr blokland for his very interesting report. it is clear that a society without batteries has become unthinkable and, as we have heard from colleagues, the use of batteries is showing an increasing trend: the world is growing at an annual rate of 9%. and by the way, let's think about all the things that we use in electronics on a daily basis as consumers. this is a good reason for all member states to make greater efforts to reduce environmental risks to a minimum in the future. it is clear that the current 1991 directive is not producing enough results on the ground, precisely because it does not contain any hard-and-fast collection and recycling objectives. moreover, it is very difficult to compare the results in the different member states. i would like to remind you of the successful example of my region, flanders, which has been mentioned many times. in flanders, the public authorities have made a huge effort, in conjunction with the industry, to set up an efficient collection system, which has resulted in no less than 60% of the batteries being effectively collected. the key factor in this success is shared responsibility. without a well-developed, dense network of collection points, ambitious collection objectives cannot be achieved. i would like to say that i would like to call for higher collection rates, which my amendment also passed in our committee. secondly, i would like to call for accountability for all actors. i have tabled a new amendment to this effect. finally, i would like to call for legal certainty for the industry, both for batteries and for the so-called power tools, with regard to cadmium use, for which i propose a transitional period of four years. i hope that many colleagues will support this.

in writing. i voted in favour of the adoption of a new european directive on batteries and accumulators and the management of their waste. the text has two objectives in a field where there are enormous disparities between the 25 member states: on the one hand, to make batteries and accumulators cleaner by considerably limiting the presence of heavy metals such as cadmium and mercury in their composition, and on the other, to organize better the collection and recycling of these products. belgium can be proud of a system which is a model for europe: a collection rate of 59%, whereas many other countries are stagnating at rates of less than 10%. the directive, drawing on the successful experience of the member states, sets ambitious collection targets: 40% after six years and 60% within ten years. these objectives cannot be achieved without a balanced policy which takes account of the whole economic chain and includes all the actors involved, from the producer to the consumer and the distributors. this is a fundamental rule for europe's future production and collection, for the benefit of the environment and consumers.
