mr president, i would like to anticipate some of the criticisms and reassure mr cousteau that there will be no media lynching of tunisia! on the contrary, it is important for us socialists to welcome, in a country which has certainly achieved impressive results in the socioeconomic field, a genuine democratic lifting of the shields by the trade unions, the human rights associations, the judiciary and lawyers, an impulse which must be able to be concretized through freedom of expression, association and assembly. this renewal is particularly evident in the committee of 18 october which will subsequently organise a permanent forum in which the socialists in this parliament wish to be associated. many people have said that the barcelona process was a semi-failure. it's true, we were afraid. europe is struggling to disseminate its pluralistic and democratic model. but today tunisia offers us a gift, almost a case study. the tunisians are not calling for our help, but they want more democracy. can we support those who, through sometimes bitter struggles, as all social struggles are, seek to open up and breathe a regime which tends to withdraw into itself, a regime which, in the name of the fight against terrorism, stigmatises all human rights defenders and prevents the judiciary and lawyers from meeting and organising? can we fight so that no socialist country can take advantage of its membership of the socialist international to trample on human rights? i have only one request: that the issue of human rights and the rule of law in tunisia be at the centre of the association council which is due to be held in january 2006 and that there be no talk of the wood.
president, madam commissioner, i have a short question in connection with product placement. it is indeed very remarkable that you are putting forward some new demands on this point. you have given the green light to product placement, and i understand that there is no other way, and you say that it should not be for children's or youth programmes, but it is not clear to me from the communication whether this also applies to non-linear supply, to the so-called on-demand supply. should that back door be closed or does the ban on product placement apply to all offers in the field of children's and youth programmes, including non-linear offers? so i would like to ask a short and clear question and i would like a clear answer.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mrs wortmann-kool, for the work she has done and, above all, for the result that is on the table today. the result, if all goes well, will be the provisional endpoint of a long and arduous negotiation. i say provisional, because there is still a lot of homework to be done on external costs, which is one of the major bottlenecks. the commission will have to develop a method of calculating external costs within two years and then a new initiative will have to be taken. some will argue that compromise amendment 37 does not go far enough. i have, of course, a lot of sympathy for that position, because i myself tabled an oral amendment in the committee on transport which goes a long way to keep the pressure on the boiler, to make faster progress on external costs, the famous 60% rule. we left this out of the compromise. it's all gonna take a little longer. this is a concession, indeed, but it is contrasted with the fact that the council, which in its common position had made no reference to external costs, has now had to make a 180° turn, and also that there is no way back: the principle of external costs in tolling will be accepted and applied. this is an important step forward towards fairer competition between modes of transport, based on the polluter pays principle. combined with the fact that the revenue from tolls will be able to be used for investment in the whole transport system and not just in roads, this means that opportunities are being created for alternative means of transport such as rail and inland waterway, and that is a great step forward.
president, commissioner frattini's reaction to the news that the cia was secretly transporting and interrogating terror suspects in europe was swift, energetic and forceful. if the european union were to act with the same energy, strength and speed against terrorism itself as it did against the alleged cia flights, we would certainly be on the right track. it is true that the fight against terrorism must be conducted within the framework of the rule of law, but the european union has a credibility problem. we are always prepared to observe a minute's silence for the victims of terrorist attacks such as those in madrid and london and even those of 11 september in new york and washington. but when it comes to taking measures and taking actions to help prevent such terrorist attacks in the future, we are not going to give up, we are primarily interested in safeguarding the rights of the terror suspects. the selective indignation in the european parliament is therefore misplaced. if the european union could produce just one concrete success in the fight against terrorism, the series of accusations against the united states might still be acceptable, but this is not the case. perhaps we should stop lecturing on morality now that it has become clear, for example, that the council will be making official contacts with organizations such as hamas and hezbollah following the palestinian parliamentary elections. hamas is not on the eu list of terrorist organisations! it is urgent that this hypocrisy be ended and that the legal framework for the fight against terrorism be adapted to the needs of today.
the president, mr president-in-office, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the accusations we are facing are extremely serious. if confirmed, they clearly constitute breaches of the principles set out in article 6 of the treaty: the european union is committed to the promotion of democracy, human rights and fundamental freedoms and the rule of law. since the last amendment to the convention, article 7 has provided for the possibility of taking action against a member state for serious breaches of these principles. such action may lead to the suspension of certain rights of that member state, including its right to vote. that is precisely why the greatest caution must be exercised. i personally think it would be too much to commit myself to the introduction of the article 7 mechanism now. of course, i too am very outraged by the alleged behaviour of the cia and i am very concerned that member states may have tolerated this behaviour, but outrage alone does not trigger a sanction mechanism. article 7 gives us, for the first time in the history of the european union, the possibility of taking action against member states' misconduct. the mechanism for this purpose may only be activated when sufficient and satisfactory elements have been collected. i trust that this parliament and its committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs and the committee on foreign affairs will do so with the utmost care.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is proof that we in the european union can make social regulations, even if we like to dress them up as economic regulations. the final result of the negotiations shows that the european parliament can make a difference and i would like to thank rapporteur schwab and mrs weiler for the way they have handled this matter. it is thanks to parliament that consumer misinformation and the excessive administrative burden on our companies due to other than c1 labelling will no longer be possible. parliament also ensured that the most dangerous machinery could be subject to real controls and that this would not be a formality, but it is now up to the member states to make this directive into something real, an ambitious working instrument. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the market is not distorted, and that the member states are not forced to take action to ensure that the market is not distorted. the european commission, commissioner, should, in my view, monitor closely the commitment of the member states, check whether they are carrying out this market control and, if necessary, take action in the future if it appears that they are not keeping their promises, and this in the interests of workers, consumers and producers. we are now at the end of our work and i think we will be able to adopt this directive tomorrow.
president. - one of the problems with the continuing violations of human rights in communist china is the lack of a credible, consistent and coherent protest by the european union. we always give lip service to human rights, but we don't act on our words. the european union and most of its member states are more interested in china's economic potential than in human rights. the european council has tried to lift the arms embargo against china. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the situation in china. i was also ashamed to learn some time ago that the belgian government had refused the dalai lama entry to its territory because it would be bad for our relations with communist china. ladies and gentlemen, we are now talking about the situation in tibet and hong kong, but we could add the threats to taiwan or the existence of the lao gai concentration camps in china. it is time for europe to send a strong signal, for example by inviting the dalai lama to this european parliament.
the european parliament has been asked to give its opinion on the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. a collection of critical journalists and citizens, diligent ngos and non-profit organizations, who, as spokespersons of civil society, uncoerced and uncensored control the state, the government, the administration and the parliament. not like this in russia. people or groups, businessmen and journalists who are too critical of the kremlin and putin's policies are silenced. as icing on the cake, the russian government now wants to silence domestic and foreign ngos as well. i am very pleased that six of the eight groups have succeeded in putting forward a joint resolution which puts the dots on the i. it is a support for people like mrs alekseyeva and mr gari kasparov. it supports the many who are active in ngos, such as alexander nikitin at belona, and ngos such as immemorial, greenpeace, social verdict and the civil society contact group. it also supports the words of my former co-chairman of the european parliament's committee on cooperation with russian parliaments, mr vladimir lukin, who clearly condemns the draft legislation in question. the resolution before us, mr president, deserves our support throughout the house.
